# Definitions of Faithfulness, Counterfactual and Monitorability

> **Work in progress (October 31, 2026).** A second pass re-read the full texts and checked every borrowed definition whose source is in the map against that source. The catalogue now holds 3,435 definitions from 216 entries. The Counterfactual and Monitorability sections are rewritten from it. Their claims are being checked against the papers and will be revised. The introduction, the main findings and the Faithfulness section are still from the first pass, which clustered 739 of the 1,974 faithfulness definitions. The other 1,235 appear under "No cluster" in the Definitions tab.

This page collects how the papers in the map define three terms: *faithfulness*, *counterfactual* and *monitorability*. It rests on 3,435 definitions quoted from 216 of the 217 entries. Each definition is quoted verbatim with its location in the paper, its kind (informal, formal, operational, criterion, failure mode or taxonomy) and its stance: proposed by the paper, or adopted or adapted from earlier work, with the source. The full set is [`definitions.csv`](https://github.com/Dundalia/faithfulness-review/blob/main/definitions.csv), one row per definition, and the Definitions tab of the dashboard lets you filter it.

For each term, the definitions are grouped into clusters of meaning. Each section then traces the lineage of the borrowed definitions, checks attributions against the cited sources, and lists the points on which the clusters disagree. In the tables, text in quotation marks is copied from the paper and text without quotation marks is a gloss.

| Term | Definitions | Entries | Clusters | Main split |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [Faithfulness](#faithfulness) | 1,974 | 204 | 8 (first pass) | One umbrella phrase over six testable properties, which can disagree on the same trace |
| [Counterfactual](#counterfactual) | 292 | 87 | 12 | What is varied (input, reasoning, internal state), whether the output must change, and what the comparison judges |
| [Monitorability](#monitorability) | 1,169 | 108 | 18 | A monitor's ability to predict a property, or a property of the trace itself |

## Main findings

**Faithfulness has one definition in words and six in practice.** Fifty entries borrow from [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards), for whom a faithful interpretation "accurately represents the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction". The tests built under that phrase measure different properties: whether the trace discloses the factors that changed the answer, whether the answer causally depends on the trace, whether trace and answer agree, whether the explanation lets an observer predict the model, whether it marks the inputs that matter, and whether it matches the model's internal computation. These verdicts can conflict. An answer that survives a corrupted chain of thought is unfaithful under the causal-dependence tests ([Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring)) and good behavior for [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan). An unverbalized cue that changed the answer is the central case of unfaithfulness for [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) and an incomplete account, not an unfaithful one, for [Zaman & Srivastava 2025](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought).

**A counterfactual is almost always a paired run.** The model is run on an original and on a variant that differs from it in one specified respect, and the two outputs are compared. The definitions differ in what is varied (the input, the model's own reasoning or an internal state), in whether the variant must change the output to count, and in what the comparison is used to judge (an explanation, the model's reliance on a feature, or a training target).

**Monitorability is defined relative to a monitor and often cited as a faithfulness requirement.** Most definitions make it a relation between a model and a reader: whether a given monitor can predict a specified property of the model's behavior from what the model exposes ([Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring)). A second group makes it a property of the trace alone, its faithful and complete verbalization of what drove the answer, and cites for this the sources that reject it. [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain), borrowed by 19 entries, never defines monitorability and writes that the chain of thought "does not need to completely represent the actual reasoning process". [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring), borrowed by 26, requires faithfulness only with respect to the property being monitored.

**The three terms meet in the same experiments.** Cue insertion and edits to the reasoning trace serve as counterfactuals in one paper, faithfulness tests in another and monitorability evaluations in a third. Some papers merge two of the terms. [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) calls a chain faithful "when an LLM monitor can tell from it that the hint influenced the answer", [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) measure faithfulness with a reasoning simulator that is "interchangeable with reasoning monitor", and [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) treat faithfulness evaluations as a proxy measure for monitorability. [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) state that their notion of monitorability "does not require a model’s externalized reasoning to faithfully reflect its internal reasoning process".

**Borrowed definitions are often misattributed.** The synthesis checked 31 attributions against the cited sources, choosing the suspected mismatches that matter most. 22 were confirmed, 5 were partly supported and 4 could not be settled. Examples: "parametric faithfulness" credited to [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning), which never uses the term (it is from [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring)); counterfactual simulatability credited to [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted), whose simulator only predicts the output on the explained input; a hint setting credited to [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring), which has no hint experiments. The checks targeted suspected mismatches, so these counts are not an error rate for the literature.

## How this page was made

1. **Excerpts.** `scripts/definitions.py excerpt` keeps each section of a paper's cached full text that mentions faithful\*, counterfactual\*, monitorab\*, legib\*, obfuscat\*, steganograph\* or "encoded reasoning", without the reference list and most appendices. A pilot on nine papers showed that keeping only the paragraphs that use a term loses definitions written as list items or named properties, so whole sections are kept.
2. **Extraction.** Claude agents, each with about twelve papers, recorded every passage that says what one of the terms or a companion term means. Each agent checked its quotes with `scripts/definitions.py check`. All 1,197 quotes are found verbatim in the full texts, up to whitespace and curly quotes.
3. **Synthesis.** One agent per term grouped the definitions into clusters (every definition belongs to exactly one, recorded in the `cluster` column), traced the lineage, checked up to ten attributions against the source texts, and wrote the section below. The raw outputs are in [`runs/D_definitions/`](https://github.com/Dundalia/faithfulness-review/tree/main/runs/D_definitions).

The extraction and the clusters are machine-made. Check a quote in the paper before citing it, and read the clusters as one grouping of the data rather than a taxonomy the papers share. Sources outside the map were not checked, including Atanasova et al. (2023), which twelve entries borrow for the counterfactual test of explanations.

## Faithfulness

The extraction holds 740 definitions in the faithfulness family, from 168 of the 217 papers. 421 define faithfulness or unfaithfulness itself; 319 define a companion term that the paper ties to it, such as plausibility, simulatability, self-consistency, post-hoc rationalization or verbalization. By kind there are 210 informal definitions, 182 operational ones, 123 criteria, 78 failure modes, 75 formal definitions and 72 taxonomies; by stance 402 are proposed, 261 adopted and 77 adapted. In one sentence: most papers name [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards)'s "accurately represents the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction" as what faithfulness means, but the tests built under that phrase measure six different properties (disclosure of influential factors, causal dependence of the answer on the reasoning, agreement between reasoning and answer, predictive value to an observer, marking of important inputs, and correspondence with internal computation), and these can give opposite verdicts on the same trace.

| Cluster | Entries | Papers | Proposed / adopted / adapted |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Accurate representation of the reasoning process](#accurate-representation-of-the-reasoning-process) | 126 | 93 | 38 / 83 / 5 |
| [Disclosure of the factors that drove the answer](#disclosure-of-the-factors-that-drove-the-answer) | 137 | 53 | 74 / 45 / 18 |
| [Causal dependence of the answer on the stated reasoning](#causal-dependence-of-the-answer-on-the-stated-reasoning) | 183 | 65 | 109 / 51 / 23 |
| [Agreement between the stated reasoning and the answer or action](#agreement-between-the-stated-reasoning-and-the-answer-or-action) | 95 | 43 | 80 / 9 / 6 |
| [Predictive value: the explanation lets an observer predict the model](#predictive-value-the-explanation-lets-an-observer-predict-the-model) | 72 | 34 | 33 / 30 / 9 |
| [Input importance: the explanation marks the inputs the prediction depends on](#input-importance-the-explanation-marks-the-inputs-the-prediction-depends-on) | 45 | 15 | 21 / 14 / 10 |
| [Correspondence with internal computation](#correspondence-with-internal-computation) | 40 | 15 | 32 / 3 / 5 |
| [Companion notions defined by contrast](#companion-notions-defined-by-contrast) | 42 | 31 | 15 / 26 / 1 |

### Accurate representation of the reasoning process

This is the umbrella answer: an explanation or CoT is faithful when it accurately represents, reflects or describes the process by which the model reached its output. Most members state the target without saying how to test it (79 of the 126 are informal, 83 adopted). The cluster also holds the 30 taxonomies that split the umbrella into kinds of test (contextual vs parametric, behavioural vs mechanistic, verbalization vs intervention, interpretability vs NLG faithfulness); they classify ways of operationalizing the umbrella rather than proposing a new property, so they are kept here.

> Intuitively, a faithful interpretation is one that accurately represents the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction.
>
> ([Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards), §1)

Formal versions:

The only formal member is a type signature: [Zaman & Srivastava 2025](./#p=zaman2025causal) define a faithfulness metric as a real-valued function $\mathcal{F}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},M)\in\mathbb{R}$ of an input, output, explanation and model, and evaluate candidate metrics by *diagnosticity*, how often a metric prefers a faithful over an unfaithful explanation, with the two built by knowledge editing so that each explanation is unfaithful to the model that produced the other.

### Disclosure of the factors that drove the answer

Here a trace is faithful when it states the factors that actually moved the answer. The standard test inserts a cue (a suggested answer, a metadata hint, reordered options), keeps the cases where the answer moves to the cued option, and asks whether the CoT mentions the cue and, in the stricter versions, whether it presents the cue as a reason. Variants extend the test from planted cues to concepts ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk); [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases)), to self-reports of which factors mattered ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit)), and to pairs of logically opposite questions answered with unverbalized bias ([Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain)).

> In order to be faithful in this setup, models must either acknowledge any biases affecting their predictions or give predictions unaffected by bias.
>
> ([Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), §2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition (short quote) |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | faithfulness under biasing features | criterion | “models must either acknowledge any biases affecting their predictions or give predictions unaffected by bias” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | systematic unfaithfulness | informal | “whether these features are an important driver of model predictions, despite their explanations not mentioning the influence of these features” |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | verbalizing the hint | criterion | “(a) the CoT mentions the hint (in part or in whole), and (b) the CoT depends on the hint to reach the answer” |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | unfaithful CoT (upper-bound argument) | criterion | “a CoT that does not verbalize the hint while using the hint is clearly unfaithful, but a CoT that verbalizes the hint is not necessarily faithful” |
| [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | articulation (of a cue) | criterion | “The model’s response has to describe the switching effect of the cue to qualify as an articulation.” |

Formal versions:

- [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning), over pairs of an unhinted prompt $x_u$ and a hinted prompt $x_h$ with answers $a_u,a_h$ and hinted CoT $c_h$:
  $$\mathrm{Faith}(M)=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{1}[c_h\text{ verbalizes the hint }h]\;\big|\;a_u\neq h,\ a_h=h\big],$$
  normalized by $\alpha=1-q/((n-2)p)$ to discount answer changes that random switching would produce, and clipped at 1.
- [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk), causal concept faithfulness on a question $\mathbf{x}$ with concepts $\mathcal{C}$: $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x})=\mathrm{PCC}\big(\mathrm{CE}(\mathbf{x},\mathcal{C}),\,\mathrm{EE}(\mathbf{x},\mathcal{C})\big)$, the Pearson correlation between each concept's causal effect on the answer and its explanation-implied effect; the dataset score is the mean of $\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x})$.
- [Yang et al. 2025](./#p=yang2025investigating): with $Y_{\mathrm{cue}}=1$ when the answer flips to the cued option and $V=1$ when the cue is verbalized, unfaithfulness is the discrepancy between the two, and the case of interest is $Y_{\mathrm{cue}}=1,\ V=0$.
- [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases): a concept $c$ is an unverbalized bias if (1) over discordant pairs of $+/-$ variants of the concept $|D^{+}|\neq|D^{-}|$ by a McNemar test, and (2) the verbalization rate on those pairs is at most $\tau$ ($\tau=0.3$).

### Causal dependence of the answer on the stated reasoning

Here a trace is faithful to the extent that the answer depends on it: truncating it, inserting a mistake, paraphrasing it, replacing it with filler, unlearning a step from the weights or patching in a chain from another problem should change the answer. Post-hoc reasoning, produced after the answer is already fixed, is the central failure, and this cluster holds most of the failure-mode entries (37 of 183: post-hoc rationalization, reasoning theater, performative or decorative CoT, shortcuts that bypass the chain). [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring)'s tests are the template; later work recasts them in mediation terms (the instruction should act on the answer only through the CoT) or moves the intervention into the parameters ([Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring)'s parametric faithfulness).

> A reasoning trace is faithful if the model’s answer causally depends on the intermediate reasoning steps.
>
> ([Swaroop et al. 2025](./#p=swaroop2025lift), §2)

|---|---|---|---|
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | adding mistakes | operational | “If inserting a mistake into the CoT changes the model’s final answer, then the model is likely not ignoring the CoT.” |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | early answering AOC | operational | “early answering AOC, a measure of post-hoc reasoning (higher is less post-hoc, indicating greater faithfulness)” |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | post-hoc reasoning (relation to faithfulness) | criterion | “Reasoning not being post-hoc does not guarantee faithfulness, nor does being post-hoc exclude faithfulness.” |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | filler tokens test | operational | “If the filler tokens provide a significant performance improvement, then the CoT may be unfaithful” |
| [Lobo et al. 2024](./#p=lobo2024impact) | faithfulness (of CoT reasoning) | informal | “the degree to which the CoT reasoning steps influence the final answer, rather than being post-hoc or unrelated” |
| [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | ff-hard | formal | “measuring whether unlearning any step causes the model to output a different label as the most likely one” |

| [Jia et al. 2026](./#p=jia2026faithfulness) | CoT faithfulness (information flow) | informal | “faithful reasoning requires that the prompt influence the answer through the mediated path $P\rightarrow C\rightarrow A$” |
| [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) | faithful model (to intermediate structures) | informal | “We define a model as faithful if the final decision causally depends on the intermediate representation.” |

Formal versions:

- [Jia et al. 2026](./#p=jia2026faithfulness) split the requirement on a trace $(P,C,A)$ (prompt, CoT, answer) into three properties: *sufficiency*, $H(A\mid C)$ is low; *completeness*, $I(P;A\mid C)\approx 0$, so that $C$ screens off $P$ from $A$ (an unverbalized hint is a completeness failure, which ties this cluster to the disclosure one); *necessity*, perturbing $C$ changes $A$.
- [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026case), with instruction $Z$, CoT $X$ and answer $Y$: the process is faithful if $p(x,y\mid z)=p(x\mid z)\,p(y\mid x)$, equivalently $Y\perp Z\mid X$.
- [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making): $\mathrm{IE}=\mathbb{E}[Y_{00}-Y_{01}]$, the indirect effect of the input on the output through the reasoning, with the input held at $X_0$ and the reasoning swapped for the chain $R_1$ generated for an intervened problem $X_1$.
- [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring): $\mathrm{ff}_{\text{hard}}=\mathbb{1}\big[\exists\,r_i:\ y\neq y^{(i)*}\big]$ and $\mathrm{ff}^{(i)}_{\text{soft}}=p(y\mid M)-p(y\mid M^{(i)*})$, where $M^{(i)*}$ is the model after unlearning step $r_i$ and $y^{(i)*}$ its prediction.
- [Swaroop et al. 2025](./#p=swaroop2025lift): $\mathrm{Faithfulness}(r_i)=\log p(a\mid q,\mathbf r)-\log p(a\mid q,\mathbf r^{(i)})$, with the continuation regenerated after step $r_i$ is modified; the trace score is the mean over steps.

### Agreement between the stated reasoning and the answer or action

Here faithfulness is a relation read off the output: the answer, or for agents the action, follows from, is entailed by, or agrees with what the reasoning says. Members either build systems in which a solver or a selection–inference pipeline derives the answer from the stated chain (faithful by construction), or measure reasoning–answer, thought–action or plan–execution consistency, or also require the reasoning to be valid, so that a correct answer reached through a flawed chain counts as unfaithful. Most are proposed (80 of 95); only 15 borrow from a source.

> To our knowledge, this is the first system to generate multistep chains that are both faithful (the answer follows from the reasoning) and truthful (the chain reflects the system’s own internal beliefs).
>
> ([Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer), Abstract)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition (short quote) |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer) | faithful chain of reasoning | informal | “both faithful (the answer follows from the reasoning) and truthful (the chain reflects the system’s own internal beliefs)” |
| [Creswell & Shanahan 2022](./#p=creswell2022faithful) | faithful reasoning | informal | “a system that reasons faithfully is one whose underlying computations mirror standard definitions of logical validity” |
| [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | Faithful CoT (faithful by construction) | criterion | “$C$ is guaranteed to be a faithful model explanation, since our final $A$ is the result of deterministically executing $C_{SL}$” |
| [Pan et al. 2023](./#p=pan2023logiclm) | faithful reasoning (symbolic solver) | criterion | “the reasoning is guaranteed to be faithful as long as the problem formulation is correct” |
| [Sanyal et al. 2022](./#p=sanyal2022fairr) | faithful reasoning (proof-to-inference causality) | criterion | “there is a causal relation from determining the proof to then generating the conclusion” |
| [Hong et al. 2023](./#p=hong2023faithful) | faithful reasoning steps (answer follows steps) | informal | “the answer may not faithfully follow the intermediate steps” |
| [Zhao et al. 2026](./#p=zhao2026robustness) | faithfulness (reasoning-answer consistency) | informal | “whether the model’s final answer is consistent with its generated chain-of-thought reasoning” |
| [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better) | unfaithful CoT (CoT-answer inconsistency) | criterion | “If an incorrect CoT induces a correct answer or a correct CoT induces a wrong answer, it is seen as an unfaithful CoT” |
| [Xu et al. 2024](./#p=xu2024faithful) | faithful / unfaithful reasoning (instance) | operational | “’faithful’ if both the answer and the process are correct and logical; ’unfaithful’ if the answer is correct but the process is not” |
| [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) | in-distribution (ID) faithfulness | formal | “whether the model’s decision matches what its predicted mediator deterministically implies” |
| [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) | reasoning faithfulness | formal | “reasoning-faithful on $x$ if and only if both outputs are stance-consistent and the reasoning has causal influence” |
| [Zhao & Daumé 2025](./#p=zhao2025necessary) | explanation inconsistency | failure mode | “failing to explicate why the model predicted $\bm{a}$ instead of its negation $\lnot\bm{a}$” |
| [Hu et al. 2026](./#p=hu2026faithful) | thought-action consistency | operational | “evaluates whether the predicted action faithfully instantiates the intent stated in the intermediate reasoning” |
| [Bu et al. 2026](./#p=bu2026spadebench) | plan-action divergence (agent deception) | informal | “a spontaneous inconsistency between an agent’s declared plan (what it says it will do) and its executed action (what it actually does)” |
| [Shiromani & Richter 2026](./#p=shiromani2026false) | action-faithful (narration) | informal | “action-faithful when every factual claim it makes about commands, files and outputs is borne out by the trajectory” |

Formal versions:

- [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking), with a deterministic map $C$ from the generated mediator to a decision: $F_{\mathrm{ID}}=\frac1N\sum_i\mathbb{1}[C(\hat m_i)=\hat y_i]$. Their stronger score, $F_{\mathrm{Strong}}=\frac1N\sum_i\mathbb{1}[C(\hat m_i)=\hat y_i\wedge C(m^{*}_i)=\hat y^{*}_i]$ with $m^{*}_i$ an intervened mediator, is interventional and is filed under causal dependence; the gap $\Delta=F_{\mathrm{ID}}-F_{\mathrm{Strong}}$ counts models that agree with their mediator but ignore interventions on it.
- [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval): $\mathrm{RF}(o,o')=\mathbb{1}[\chi(o)=1\wedge\chi(o')=1\wedge\kappa(o,o')=1]$, where stance consistency $\chi(o)=\bigwedge_i\iota(c_{1:i-1},c_i)$ requires each component of the output to keep the prior stance or justify leaving it, and causal influence $\kappa(o,o')=\mathbb{1}[S(r_{\mathrm{new}})\neq S(r)]\vee\mathbb{1}[S(a')\neq S(a)]$ after counterfactual reasoning $r'$ is prefilled. The definition is a conjunction of this cluster's property and the previous one's.
- [Zhao & Daumé 2025](./#p=zhao2025necessary), prediction–explanation consistency: $C(e)=\log\dfrac{M(e\mid q,a)}{M(e\mid q,\lnot a)}$, how much better the explanation supports the prediction than its negation.
- [Creswell & Shanahan 2022](./#p=creswell2022faithful): a trace is *valid* if it is *connected* (every statement a step selects is in the context or was inferred by an earlier step) and each step $r_k=\langle s,i\rangle$ is correct, i.e. $i$ follows logically from $s$.

### Predictive value: the explanation lets an observer predict the model

Here an explanation is faithful if a reader, whether a human, a simulator model or the model itself in another context, can use it to predict what the model does on the same input or on related ones. The cluster runs from forward simulation and leakage-adjusted simulatability through counterfactual simulatability to normalized simulatability gain, and also includes the self-report literature, where a model's description of its own preferences or tendencies is faithful if it matches measured behaviour, and the self-consistency framing of [Pres et al. 2026](./#p=pres2026consistency), in which faithful self-description is a consistency relation between what a model says about its behaviour and what it does. When the related inputs are counterfactuals of the original input, papers call the property counterfactual simulatability ([Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl); [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm)); definitions of counterfactual simulatability as such are catalogued in the [Counterfactual](#counterfactual) section, and the entries here are those that state it as faithfulness.

> A faithful explanation should help an observer predict how the model will behave on related inputs.
>
> ([Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm), §2.1)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition (short quote) |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | fidelity | operational | “the degree to which the explanation model can mimic the original model’s decisions” |
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | unfaithful interpretation (decision mismatch) | criterion | “An interpretation is unfaithful if it results in different decisions than the model it interprets.” |
| [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | simulatability | informal | “A model is simulatable to the extent that an observer, or simulator, can predict its outputs.” |
| [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott) | faithfulness (LAS) | operational | “To evaluate the faithfulness of the rationales generated by the student, we use LAS” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | simulatability | informal | “simulatability is a special case of faithfulness, which requires the output predictor to be humans rather than arbitrary black-box models” |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | simulatability | informal | “Simulatability is a stricter form of faithfulness that requires CoTs to be both faithful and legible to humans.” |
| [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) | explanation faithfulness (simulatability framework) | informal | “deemed faithful if, upon reading the explanation, it allows humans to correctly anticipate the model’s behavior” |
| [Sherburn et al. 2024](./#p=sherburn2024language) | faithful explanation | informal | “faithful if it accurately describes the model’s behavior on a sufficiently wide range of held out in-distribution and out-of-distribution examples” |
| [Malladi & Srivastava 2026](./#p=malladi2026directly) | behavioral faithfulness | informal | “whether an explanation enables accurate reproduction of a model’s observable input–output behavior” |
| [Sivakumaran et al. 2026](./#p=sivakumaran2026balancing) | faithfulness (multi-party / reasoning executability) | informal | “a faithful explanation is one which enables the listener model to come to the same conclusion as the speaker, without access to the speaker’s answer” |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | faithfulness metric (reasoning simulator) | formal | “We say a CoT is faithful based on the accuracy of the “reasoning simulator”” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | faithful explanation (predictive principle) | informal | “A faithful explanation should help an observer predict how the model will behave on related inputs.” |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | faithful with respect to v | formal | “faithful with respect to $v$ if it contains all relevant information to predicting $v$ , i.e. $z$ is a sufficient statistic for $v$” |
| [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) | faithfulness (preference self-report metric) | operational | “the degree to which the model’s self-report describes its true decision-making behavior, regardless of whether that behavior matches the training target” |
| [Pres et al. 2026](./#p=pres2026consistency) | faithful self-description | informal | “both faithful self-description and self-improvement are special cases of self-consistency” |
