Formal versions:

- [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted), with leakage indicator $k_i=\mathbb{1}[\hat y_i\mid\hat e_i]$ (the simulator recovers the label from the explanation alone):
  $$\mathrm{LAS}_k=\operatorname*{mean}_{i:\,k_i=k}\Big(\mathbb{1}[\hat y_i\mid x_i,\hat e_i]-\mathbb{1}[\hat y_i\mid x_i]\Big),\qquad \mathrm{LAS}=\tfrac12(\mathrm{LAS}_0+\mathrm{LAS}_1).$$
- [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm): $\mathrm{NSG}=\dfrac{\mathrm{Acc}_{\text{with}}-\mathrm{Acc}_{\text{without}}}{1-\mathrm{Acc}_{\text{without}}}$, the predictor's accuracy gain from the explanation on related inputs, normalized by the largest possible gain; 1 is read as perfectly faithful, 0 as no predictive benefit, negative values as misleading.
- [Zaman & Srivastava 2025](./#p=zaman2025causal), after Chan et al. (2022a): simulatability $\mathbb{1}_S(y_i\mid x_i,\varepsilon_i)-\mathbb{1}_S(y_i\mid x_i)$, the accuracy gain of simulator $S$ from seeing the explanation.
- [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring): a latent $z$ (e.g. a CoT) is faithful with respect to an output property $v$ if $z$ is a sufficient statistic for $v$; the definition makes no claim about the causal role of $z$.
- [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying): $\operatorname{corr}(\hat{\boldsymbol p},\tilde{\boldsymbol p})$ between the model's self-reported and its measured preference parameters.

### Input importance: the explanation marks the inputs the prediction depends on

This is the feature-attribution answer carried over from pre-LLM interpretability: an explanation is faithful if the input parts it marks as important are the ones whose removal or alteration changes the prediction (erasure, comprehensiveness, sufficiency), or if the inputs that drive the prediction also drive the explanation (attribution agreement, CC-SHAP). Self-explanation work applies the same logic by editing the input according to the model's own attribution, redaction or counterfactual explanation and checking whether the prediction flips ([Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self); [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating)). The cluster is small (45 entries from 15 papers) and is cited by the CoT literature mainly through [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards)'s assumptions and [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring)'s CC-SHAP.

> rationales extracted for an instance in this case ought to have meaningfully influenced its prediction for the same. We call these faithful rationales.
>
> ([DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser), §4.2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition (short quote) |
|---|---|---|---|
| [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser) | comprehensiveness | operational | “We construct a contrast example for $x_{i}$ , $\tilde{x}_{i}$ , which is $x_{i}$ with the predicted rationales $r_{i}$ removed.” |
| [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser) | sufficiency | operational | “the degree to which the snippets within the extracted rationales are adequate for a model to make a prediction” |
| [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser) | faithful by construction (hard rationale selection) | criterion | “We view these as inherently faithful, because by construction we know which snippets the decoder used to make a prediction.” |
| [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) | faithful explanation (attention) | criterion | “(i) Attention weights should correlate with feature importance measures (e.g., gradient-based measures); (ii) Alternative (or counterfactual) attention weight configurations ought to yield corresponding changes in prediction” |
| [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention) | faithful explanation (attention; exclusivity) | criterion | “if alternative attention distributions exist that produce similar results to those obtained by the original model, then the original model’s attention scores cannot be reliably used to “faithfully” explain the model’s prediction” |
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | unfaithful interpretation system (Model Assumption) | criterion | “An interpretation system is unfaithful if it results in different interpretations of models that make the same decisions.” |
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | faithful heat-map interpretation (Linearity Assumption) | criterion | “the contributions of different parts of the input are independent from each other” |
| [Madsen et al. 2023](./#p=madsen2023faithfulness) | erasure-metric definition of importance | criterion | “if information (tokens) is truly important, then masking them should result in worse model performance compared to masking random information (tokens)” |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) | faithful redaction explanation | criterion | “The explanation is faithful if the model can not classify the redacted paragraph.” |
| [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) | faithful self-explanation (self-consistency check) | criterion | “a self-explanation is considered faithful if editing the input according to it indeed flips the prediction” |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | unfaithfulness (perturbing unimportant features) | operational | “observe different answers on perturbing unimportant features identified by an explanation” |
| [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring) | feature importance agreement | operational | “input tokens important for label prediction should be important for rationale generation and vice versa” |
| [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | faithful explanation (input-contribution condition) | criterion | “the tokens given as input to the model contribute similarly to the model’s answer prediction and to the explanation it generates to justify its prediction” |
| [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) | CC-Sp (attribution-based self-consistency) | operational | “Spearman rank correlation captures agreement in feature prioritization while abstracting from the attribution scale” |
| [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) | faithfulness of self-generated counterfactuals | operational | “the model predictions on SCEs do not yield the target label, meaning that self-generated counterfactual reasoning does not align with model predictions” |

And 30 more entries, from 13 papers: [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal), [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser), [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention), [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self), [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards), [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning), [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating), [Madsen et al. 2023](./#p=madsen2023faithfulness), [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention), [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring), [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did), [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating), [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring).

Formal versions:

- [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser), with $m(\cdot)_j$ the predicted probability of class $j$ and $r_i$ the rationale for $x_i$: $\mathrm{comprehensiveness}=m(x_i)_j-m(x_i\setminus r_i)_j$ (high when erasing the rationale hurts) and $\mathrm{sufficiency}=m(x_i)_j-m(r_i)_j$ (low when the rationale alone suffices). [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) apply KL versions to CoT tokens, $\mathrm{COMP}=\mathrm{KL}\big(p(Y\mid x,T)\,\|\,p(Y\mid x,T\setminus R)\big)$ and $\mathrm{SUFF}=\mathrm{KL}\big(p(Y\mid x,T)\,\|\,p(Y\mid x,R)\big)$.
- [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring): $\text{CC-SHAP}=1-\mathrm{DIV}\big(C(P)\,\|\,C(E)\big)$, with $C(P)$ and $C(E)$ the per-token Shapley contribution ratios of the input to the prediction and to the explanation and DIV the cosine distance; the value lies in $[-1,1]$ and the authors call it a self-consistency measure, not a faithfulness measure.

### Correspondence with internal computation

Here faithfulness is checked against the model's internals rather than its behaviour: a CoT step is faithful if it describes a process that took place in the model, as shown by probes, attribution graphs, circuits or concept-level ablations. Several members present this as the full notion that behavioural tests only approximate ([Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative); [Anonymous 2026, *GRACE*](./#p=anon2026grace)), and the introspection papers use the same idea under the name grounding: a self-report must share a causal mechanism with the behaviour it reports, not only match it ([Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying)). One member applies the word to a different object: [Lindsey et al. 2025](./#p=lindsey2025biology) ask whether a transcoder replacement is a causally faithful model of the MLP it replaces.

> A CoT step is faithful iff it accurately describes a process that took place at some point in the model.
>
> ([Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness), Def. 1 (§2.2))

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition (short quote) |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) | CoT step faithfulness | informal | “A CoT step is faithful iff it accurately describes a process that took place at some point in the model.” |
| [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness) | CoT faithfulness | informal | “A CoT is faithful iff (1) it contains a complete reasoning path the model followed to reach its answer, and (2) it contains no unfaithful steps.” |
| [Lindsey et al. 2025](./#p=lindsey2025biology) | chain-of-thought faithfulness (three cases) | taxonomy | “cases where the model genuinely performs the steps it says it is performing, cases where it makes up its reasoning without regard for truth, and cases where it works backwards from a human-provided clue” |
| [Lindsey et al. 2025](./#p=lindsey2025biology) | mechanistic faithfulness (replacement model) | informal | “it is not guaranteed that they learn a causally faithful model of the original MLP” |
| [Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative) | full mechanistic faithfulness | informal | “full mechanistic faithfulness, which would require asking whether the CoT reflects the model’s internal computation” |
| [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026verbalising) | CoT faithfulness | informal | “CoT is faithful when its visible rationale consistently reflects how reasoning-relevant information is computed in the model’s internal decision-making process” |
| [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026concept) | faithfulness as internal concept grounding | informal | “does an LLM’s CoT engage the same internal concepts that also support the LLM’s direct prediction, and do the shared concepts causally drive its answer?” |
| [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) | NeuroFaith faithfulness | formal | “the proportion of concepts it contains being mechanistically important, i.e. having positive attribution score” |
| [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) | faithful reasoning trace | criterion | “faithful reasoning traces should align with the model’s internal computational process” |
| [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) | faithful CoT (to internal representation) | criterion | “regardless of whether the answer is correct, since CIA measures faithfulness rather than correctness” |
| [Anonymous 2026, *Computation Alignment*](./#p=anon2026computation) | computation alignment | informal | “the computation process by which the student produces the final answer should be consistent with the problem-solving steps described in its CoT” |
| [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) | grounding | informal | “Grounding requires reporting the true cause.” |
| [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) | correct confabulator | failure mode | “a correct confabulator: accurately self-reporting through an ungrounded mechanism” |
| [Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable) | decodability (necessary condition for faithfulness) | criterion | “Decodability is a necessary condition for faithfulness” |
| [Anonymous 2026, *GRACE*](./#p=anon2026grace) | behavioural CoT faithfulness measurement (limitation) | criterion | “cannot separate a model whose answer is supported by a different mechanism than its CoT suggests from one whose answer is simply robust to perturbation” |

And 25 more entries, from 14 papers: [Bhupatiraju & Nyaupane 2026](./#p=bhupatiraju2026stated), [Lindsey et al. 2025](./#p=lindsey2025biology), [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying), [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness), [Li et al. 2025](./#p=li2025training), [Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable), [Anonymous 2026, *GRACE*](./#p=anon2026grace), [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting), [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026concept), [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026verbalising), [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did), [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making), [Anonymous 2026, *Introspection or Post-Hoc Rationalization?*](./#p=anon2026introspection), [Anonymous 2026, *Computation Alignment*](./#p=anon2026computation).

Formal versions:

- [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did), NeuroFaith: for an explanation $e(x)$ containing concepts $c_1,\dots,c_p$ and an interpreter attribution $I_\Gamma$ along circuit $\Gamma$, $F(x,e)=\frac1p\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathbb{1}_{I_\Gamma(c_i)>0}$.
- [Anonymous 2026, *Computation Alignment*](./#p=anon2026computation), computation alignment of a sample $s$: $A(s)=\dfrac{|N^{*}_{r}\cap N^{*}_{y}|}{|N^{*}_{r}\cup N^{*}_{y}|}$, the node overlap between attribution circuits traced from a reasoning-critical and an answer-critical token.

### Companion notions defined by contrast

These entries define a companion term in order to say what faithfulness is not: 23 of the 42 concern plausibility, how convincing an explanation is to humans; 8 define self-consistency in [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring)'s sense, agreement between explanation and prediction at the output level, which they argue is all that input- and CoT-perturbation tests measure; the remainder are consistency with gold labels or across similar inputs, truthfulness and belief, honesty, effectiveness, interpretability, monitorability and mechanistic relevance. One entry ties faithfulness to monitorability explicitly ([Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight)); the monitorability definitions themselves are in the [Monitorability](#monitorability) section.

> “Plausibility” refers to how convincing the interpretation is to humans, while “faithfulness” refers to how accurately it reflects the true reasoning process of the model Herman (2017); Wiegreffe and Pinter (2019).
>
> ([Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards), §2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition (short quote) |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | plausibility | informal | ““Plausibility” refers to how convincing the interpretation is to humans” |
| [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | plausibility | informal | “plausibility (a.k.a. persuasiveness or understandability), which refers to “how convincing an explanation is to humans”” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | plausibility | informal | “Plausibility evaluates humans’ preference of an explanation based on its factual correctness and logical coherence” |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | plausibility (of self-explanations) | informal | “An explanation is considered plausible if it is coherent with human reasoning and understanding.” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | plausible (vs unfaithful) | informal | “may be coherent and consistent with its prediction on that example (in which case we call it plausible) while being misleading about how the system will make predictions on other examples” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | plausibility (necessary condition) | criterion | “the plausibility of explanations, which is necessary for faithfulness, but as we demonstrate, is insufficient for establishing it” |
| [Höth et al. 2026](./#p=hoth2026atmanrl) | plausibility | informal | “plausibility reflects consistency with an external evaluator, not alignment with the model’s internal computation” |
| [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | self-consistency (relation to faithfulness) | criterion | “Yet self-consistency is a necessary, but not sufficient test for faithfulness.” |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | self-consistency | criterion | “any “faithfulness” test which relies on executing changes to the model’s input and observing changes to the prediction actually measures self-consistency rather than faithfulness” |
| [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | self-consistency | informal | “Perturbing the reasoning chain while maintaining model parameters fixed measures self-consistency” |
| [Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer) | truthful (chain of reasoning) | informal | “truthful (the proof reflects the system’s beliefs)” |
| [Walden & Wanner 2026](./#p=walden2026reasoning) | honesty (LLM) | informal | “an LLM is said to be honest if it is aware of its capabilities (self-knowledge), and is able to faithfully express its knowledge” |
| [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better) | effectiveness (vs. faithfulness) | informal | “Effectiveness is result-oriented, focusing on whether CoT can enhance the quality of reasoning outcomes; whereas faithfulness is process-oriented” |
| [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) | faithfulness (contrasted with monitorability) | informal | “without requiring its reasoning to causally reflect the underlying decision-making, as faithfulness does” |
| [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) | faithfulness (vs. mechanistic relevance) | informal | “CoT text can causally impact behavior even if it does not straightforwardly “explain” model internals” |

And 27 more entries, from 20 papers: [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm), [Ramnath et al. 2023](./#p=ramnath2024tailoring), [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent), [Occhipinti et al. 2026](./#p=occhipinti2026probing), [Anonymous 2026, *CRISP*](./#p=anon2026crisp), [DeYoung et al. 2020](./#p=deyoung2020eraser), [Gur-Arieh et al. 2026](./#p=gurarieh2026faithfulness), [Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer), [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning), [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning), [Sun et al. 2025](./#p=sun2026refine), [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful), [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention), [Zhao & Daumé 2025](./#p=zhao2025necessary), [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026concept), [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did), [Chen et al. 2022](./#p=chen2022rev), [Cahlik et al. 2025](./#p=cahlik2025reasoninggrounded), [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring), [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training).

### Lineage

338 of the 740 entries are borrowed: 261 adopted and 77 adapted. 269 name at least one source that is in the map; 69 cite only sources outside it. Four sources, [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards), [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring), [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) and [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning), are each borrowed by 35 or more papers, and each fed a different cluster: Jacovi & Goldberg the umbrella and the plausibility contrast, Lanham et al. causal dependence, Turpin et al. and Chen et al. disclosure. The table counts, for each of the most-borrowed sources, the papers and entries that borrow from it (an entry with several sources counts for each) and the clusters those entries fall in.

| Source | Papers borrowing | Entries | Clusters of the borrowing entries |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) | 50 | 66 | umbrella 45, contrast 13, prediction 4, causal dependence 2, mechanism 1, input importance 1 |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | 43 | 59 | causal dependence 34, umbrella 20, disclosure 4, agreement 1 |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | 42 | 53 | disclosure 28, umbrella 20, causal dependence 3, input importance 1, agreement 1 |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | 35 | 46 | disclosure 34, umbrella 10, causal dependence 1, mechanism 1 |
| [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | 14 | 15 | disclosure 10, umbrella 5 |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | 11 | 12 | umbrella 5, disclosure 5, causal dependence 2 |
| [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | 11 | 14 | contrast 6, input importance 4, umbrella 3, causal dependence 1 |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | 9 | 11 | prediction 8, contrast 2, umbrella 1 |
| [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) | 7 | 7 | prediction 7 |
| Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 | 7 | 7 | prediction 6, umbrella 1 |
| [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) | 6 | 6 | agreement 3, umbrella 3 |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | 6 | 6 | umbrella 5, causal dependence 1 |
| Atanasova et al. 2023 | 6 | 7 | disclosure 2, umbrella 2, causal dependence 1, input importance 1, contrast 1 |

**Jacovi & Goldberg's sentence and its paraphrases.** 49 papers cite Jacovi & Goldberg for what faithfulness is (53 entries), and 13 more entries cite them for plausibility. Jacovi & Goldberg word it two ways, "accurately represents the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction" and "how accurately it reflects the true reasoning process of the model". 19 of the 53 entries keep "reasoning process", and only 8 keep "accurately represent". The others substitute "decision process", "decision-making process", internal computation, behaviour, factors or a causal verb. These substitutions move the target in four directions, and each direction is one of the clusters above:

- toward internal computation: "the reasoning accurately describe the models internal computation" ([Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient)), "the degree to which its reasoning traces reflect its internal decision-making" ([Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training));
- toward behaviour: "explanations should reflect how the model actually behaves, not just sound plausible" ([Malladi & Srivastava 2026](./#p=malladi2026directly)), "whether explanations support actual model behavior" ([Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted));
- toward causal role: "the degree to which a model’s stated reasoning genuinely causes its final prediction, rather than merely accompanying it" ([Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking)), "the degree to which it accurately represents or contributes to the underlying reasoning of the model" ([Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026concept));
- toward factors: "An explanation is faithful if it reflects the factors behind a model’s prediction" ([Anonymous 2026, *One Draw Is Not Enough*](./#p=anon2026one)).

[Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) adapt the sentence in two ways at once. They relax it to "the key decision factors and reasoning chains" and add a predictive clause, that these should "enable us to predict how it would process similar inputs during deployment". Their operational test then uses only the first part, and 34 disclosure entries borrow from it.

**Plausibility drifts from persuasiveness to correctness.** For Jacovi & Goldberg plausibility is "how convincing the interpretation is to humans", and [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) keep that sense ("a.k.a. persuasiveness or understandability"). [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) write instead "Plausibility evaluates humans’ preference of an explanation based on its factual correctness and logical coherence", citing Herman 2017, Lage et al. 2019 and Jacovi & Goldberg. The same sentence recurs in [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent), and [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) have "the factual correctness of a CoT and how convincingly it justifies the model’s response". [Cahlik et al. 2025](./#p=cahlik2025reasoninggrounded) reduce plausibility to whether "the explanations are logically sound", and [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) operationalize it by asking "how often is the reasoning chain truly correct". Once plausibility means correctness, it can no longer be told apart from the validity-based definitions in the agreement cluster, such as [Xu et al. 2024](./#p=xu2024faithful)'s "’faithful’ if both the answer and the process are correct and logical".

**Lanham et al.'s tests become per-trace verdicts.** [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) report early answering and adding mistakes as task-level measures of post-hoc reasoning, read them with a hedge ("the model is likely not ignoring the CoT") and state that "Reasoning not being post-hoc does not guarantee faithfulness, nor does being post-hoc exclude faithfulness." Borrowers turn the tests into labels on single traces. For early answering, [Tanneru et al. 2024](./#p=tanneru2024hardness) write that it "considers a generated CoT to be faithful if truncating that CoT causes the model to change its final response"; for adding mistakes, [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) write "If the prediction of the model changes, the CoT is considered faithful". The filler-tokens test changes its question. Lanham et al. ask whether filler tokens alone account for CoT's accuracy gain ("If the filler tokens provide a significant performance improvement, then the CoT may be unfaithful"); [Zaman & Srivastava 2025](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) instead replace the CoT with ellipses and call it "unfaithful if this corruption does not change the original prediction". One adoption reverses Lanham et al.'s caveat (see the attribution check).

**Cue tests: from counterfactual simulatability to verbalization rates.** [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) framed unfaithfulness predictively: reasoning that is plausible on one example can be "misleading about how the system will make predictions on other examples (in which case we also call it unfaithful)". They measured it by accuracy drops and by prediction pairs that change when the biasing feature is added. [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) made verbalization itself the score and required both mention and dependence, and [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) required the CoT to "describe the switching effect of the cue". Downstream the requirement splits. [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) score "the presence of the given cue in a model’s reasoning trace", and [Nguyen et al. 2026](./#p=nguyen2026generalization), "Following Meek et al. (2025)", "label a CoT trace as faithful when it mentions the injected cue". [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) asks only whether "an LLM monitor can tell from it that the hint influenced the answer". [Anonymous 2026, *One Draw Is Not Enough*](./#p=anon2026one), [egan 2026](./#p=egan2026hint) and [Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms) keep both conditions. [Young 2026](./#p=young2026measuring) concludes that the resulting numbers form "a family of related constructs whose values depend critically on the measurement instrument". The predictive framing survives in [Occhipinti et al. 2026](./#p=occhipinti2026probing) ("based on the counterfactual simulatability of CoT reasoning (Turpin et al., 2023)") and in [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching), whose statement that the cue tests "operationalize the counterfactual simulatability notion of faithfulness" is catalogued in the [Counterfactual](#counterfactual) section.

**Simulatability becomes a faithfulness score.** Forward simulation (Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017, outside the map) leads to [Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted)'s leakage-adjusted simulatability. [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott), [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto), [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) and [Chen et al. 2022](./#p=chen2022rev) then use LAS directly as the faithfulness of rationales ("To evaluate the faithfulness of the rationales generated by the student, we use LAS"). [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) place simulatability under faithfulness, "a special case of faithfulness, which requires the output predictor to be humans", and are followed by [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm); [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) call it "a stricter form of faithfulness". [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm)'s normalized simulatability gain is adopted by [Pres et al. 2026](./#p=pres2026selfctrl), [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal) and [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient).

**Self-consistency and "parametric faithfulness".** [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) argue that input- and CoT-perturbation tests measure self-consistency, "a necessary, but not sufficient test for faithfulness". [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring), [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring), [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning), [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did), [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026concept) and [Anonymous 2026, *CRISP*](./#p=anon2026crisp) adopt the term in that sense. Tutek et al. use the argument to motivate parametric faithfulness, an intervention on the weights (unlearning a reasoning step), and [Zaman & Srivastava 2025](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) and [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) adopt parametric faithfulness in Tutek et al.'s sense. [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) keep both words but change both meanings (see the attribution check).

### Attribution check

269 borrowed entries name a source that is in the map (378 entry–source pairs). A coarse first pass asked whether the source's own entries, in any family, include one in the same cluster as the borrowing entry. 257 of the 269 entries pass, but this says little, because the heavily borrowed sources have entries in several clusters. Reading each borrowed quote against the source's entries gave a shorter list of wording-level candidates. These were checked in the source texts (`cache/<key>.txt`), starting with the sources borrowed by the most papers.

**Confirmed mismatches.** These are the eight with the widest reach:

1. [Lobo et al. 2024](./#p=lobo2024impact): "Following Lanham et al. (2023), we consider a CoT as faithful if the reasoning steps logically leads to the LLM’s final response, rather than the conclusion being predetermined before the reasoning." This is attributed to [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring), but Lanham et al. never give it as a definition, and "logically" and "predetermined" do not occur in their text. What they state is the opposite relation: "Reasoning not being post-hoc does not guarantee faithfulness, nor does being post-hoc exclude faithfulness." The nearest statement is "Since post-hoc reasoning does not change the model’s answer, there is no strong reason to believe that such reasoning would be faithful."
2. [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) (entries #1 and #5) name the property "parametric faithfulness" and credit it to [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) (with Barez et al. 2025, Xiong et al. 2025 and [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual)). The word "parametric" does not occur in Chen et al. 2025 or in Hase & Potts 2026. Chen et al.'s nearest wording is "The CoT $c$ is faithful if it accurately reflects $M$ ’s internal reasoning process". The term comes from [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring), where it means that unlearning a step changes the answer. Tutek et al. contrast it with contextual faithfulness, which perturbs the context, and [Sun et al. 2026](./#p=sun2026investigating) list "injecting answer hints into the context" under contextual faithfulness; Hong et al. apply the name to that hint test.
3. [Imrankhan 2026](./#p=imrankhan2026better): "The Unfaithful CoT Rate (UCR) [Turpin et al., 2023] quantifies the fraction of incorrect outputs under misleading hints whose reasoning tokens fail to verbalize the hint’s presence". [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) define no such rate. Their metrics are the accuracy drop in biased contexts, "% Unfaithful Overall", "the overall fraction of examples whose predictions change when adding the biasing feature", and the "Percentage of Unfaithfulness Explained by Bias". Verbalization was checked by hand: "we review 426 explanations supporting biased predictions and only 1 explicitly mentions the bias".
4. [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) (and the same sentence in [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) and [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning)) define plausibility by "factual correctness and logical coherence", citing Jacovi & Goldberg among others. [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) define it only as "how convincing the interpretation is to humans"; the words "factual" and "correctness", and any form of "coherence", do not occur in their text. Whether the wording comes from the co-cited Herman 2017 or Lage et al. 2019, which are outside the map, is not settled.
5. [Sanyal et al. 2022](./#p=sanyal2022fairr): "Faithfulness of such models Jacovi and Goldberg (2020) aims to understand whether the models are actually learning to solve the task or rather depending on some shortcut patterns." [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards) never mention shortcuts. The nearest statement is their definition, "a faithful interpretation is one that accurately represents the reasoning process behind the model’s prediction".
6. [Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making) (entry #2): "self-consistency tests whether a model produces a consistent explanation across multiple samples or under paraphrase (Parcalabescu & Frank, 2024; Zhao & Iii, 2025)". [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring)'s self-consistency is agreement between explanation and prediction at the output level, tested by "self-consistency tests that compare the outputs of models after modifying their inputs" or by CC-SHAP's input contributions. Sampling consistency does not appear in their text, and "paraphrase" occurs only as the name of Lanham et al.'s CoT test. The co-cited Zhao & Iii 2025 is outside the map and was not checked.
7. [Li et al. 2025](./#p=li2025drift): "Faithfulness evaluation for rationale is to apply important perturbation to the original rationale and check the changes in the new output (Parcalabescu and Frank, 2023)." This cites [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) for the view their paper argues against: "In this work we argue that these faithfulness tests do not measure faithfulness to the models’ inner workings – but rather their self-consistency at output level."
8. [Höth et al. 2026](./#p=hoth2026atmanrl): "An unfaithful reasoning trace may appear plausible and logically coherent while the model reaches the correct answer through shortcuts that bypass the stated reasoning (Agarwal et al., 2024)." [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) contain the first half, "While LLMs can generate responses that seem logical and coherent (i.e., being plausible), these explanations may not accurately reflect the model’s actual reasoning process (i.e., being faithful)", but "shortcut" and "bypass" do not occur in their text.

Two less-borrowed sources fail in the same way. [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) credit [Emmons et al. 2025](./#p=emmons2025chain) with a "faithfulness floor", a word Emmons et al. do not use; their nearest claim is that "for sufficiently difficult tasks, such as complex sabotage, CoT-as-computation becomes necessary". [Yang et al. 2025](./#p=yang2025investigating) define "Scheming: $(1-\text{VR})\cdot\text{CIR}_{a}$ , originally introduced by Turpin et al. (2025)". [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) define the same product, "$(1-\textrm{verbalization rate})\times\textrm{cue influence rate}$", but call it "the effective cue influence rate (ECR)"; "scheming" appears in their text only as a kind of behaviour.

**Checked and found in the source.** Some candidates turned out to be stated in the source text even though not among its extracted entries. [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) credit [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) with "restoration errors (models silently correct their errors without verbalization)". The cached version of Arcuschin et al. defines restoration errors in an appendix ("Negative Results for Restoration Errors"), where they report finding no evidence "other than cases of likely dataset contamination". [Occhipinti et al. 2026](./#p=occhipinti2026probing)'s attribution of "counterfactual simulatability" to Turpin et al. matches Turpin et al.'s own heading, "counterfactual simulatability framework of explanation faithfulness". [Drori et al. 2025](./#p=drori2025output)'s gloss of Chen et al. ("unfaithful if it omits important intermediate reasoning") is a fair inversion of "a faithful CoT should highlight the key decision factors and reasoning chains". [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness)'s "perturbing unimportant features identified by an explanation" is close to Turpin et al.'s test of "whether ... model predictions are insensitive to features not referenced by explanations".

**Not settled.** It is a judgement call whether [Tanneru et al. 2024](./#p=tanneru2024hardness)'s and [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring)'s per-trace readings of Lanham et al.'s tests (quoted under Lineage) misattribute the hedged, task-level original or paraphrase it acceptably. 69 borrowed entries cite only sources outside the map (for example Bao et al. 2025, the source of [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026case)'s mediation definition and of [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring)'s validity condition), and none of them was checked. Several entries credit cue omission to Lanham et al. alongside cue-test papers ([Golchin & Wetter 2026](./#p=golchin2026monitorability), [MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning), [Jose et al. 2026](./#p=jose2026strategic)). Lanham et al. run no cue tests, so these claims rest on the co-cited papers; they were not treated as mismatches.

### Tensions

1. **Is an answer that survives a corrupted chain a sign of unfaithfulness?** Under the causal-dependence tests, yes. Adding mistakes counts an unchanged answer against the trace ([Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring); [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring); [Zaman & Srivastava 2025](./#p=zaman2025causal)), and [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) require that "A faithful model, upon processing the logically inconsistent trace, should change its prediction or at least become less certain." [Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc) accept the verdict while noting its cost: answering correctly despite a flawed scratchpad "is perhaps the preferred behavior, but it would constitute unfaithful reasoning". [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) rejects it: "corruption cannot distinguish unfaithfulness from justified correction, since ignoring a broken chain is good behavior". [Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative) separate "silent bypass" from "self-correction", which a single answer-change rate merges.

2. **Is an unverbalized but influential cue unfaithfulness or incompleteness?** For the disclosure cluster it is the central case: "a CoT that does not verbalize the hint while using the hint is clearly unfaithful" ([Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning); likewise [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek), [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases)). [Zaman & Srivastava 2025](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) answer that the test "confuses unfaithfulness with incompleteness, the lossy compression needed to turn distributed transformer computation into a linear natural language narrative", and that it measures "verbalized sensitivity", which "is not the same as faithfulness". [Anonymous 2026, *One Draw Is Not Enough*](./#p=anon2026one) ("A trace that omits the cue may be incomplete rather than unfaithful") and [Nguyen et al. 2026](./#p=nguyen2026generalization) ("cue acknowledgment does not establish faithfulness in the conventional sense") take the same side.

3. **Which cue cases count, and is mention enough?** An unchanged answer is faithful for [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), whose models may "give predictions unaffected by bias", and for [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching): "If the model’s answer does not change between the prompts, we say it is faithful." [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) and [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) score only switches ("We only test for faithfulness on the switched examples"). [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) score non-switches too, and a model that does not discuss the cue counts as "not being faithful" even when its answer did not move. Mention alone suffices for [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) and [Nguyen et al. 2026](./#p=nguyen2026generalization). [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning), [egan 2026](./#p=egan2026hint) and [Anonymous 2026, *One Draw Is Not Enough*](./#p=anon2026one) also require that the CoT depend on the cue, and [Young 2026](./#p=young2026measuring) shows that the two classifiers measure different constructs ("mention" vs "epistemic dependence").

4. **Is agreement between reasoning and answer faithfulness?** The agreement cluster says yes: "we use the term faithfulness to mean whether the model’s final answer is consistent with its generated chain-of-thought reasoning" ([Zhao et al. 2026](./#p=zhao2026robustness); also [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better), [Cahlik et al. 2025](./#p=cahlik2025reasoninggrounded)). Systems whose answer is computed from the chain claim faithfulness by construction ([Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful); [Pan et al. 2023](./#p=pan2023logiclm); [Tafjord et al. 2022](./#p=tafjord2022entailer)). Others rule this out. [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) call reasoning that is "coherent and consistent with its prediction" merely plausible, and possibly unfaithful. [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) write that "self-consistency alone does not establish causal reliance", and [Emmons et al. 2025](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) list "thought-response consistency" as distinct from faithfulness. [Lyu et al. 2023](./#p=lyu2023faithful) concede the point for unconstrained chains: "there is no known inconsistency between the reasoning chain and the answer, but this still does not imply that the reasoning chain is a faithful explanation". [Zhao et al. 2026](./#p=zhao2026robustness) themselves call their measure weaker than "the stronger notion of explanation faithfulness in the interpretability literature".

5. **Behavioural tests or internals?** The causal-dependence, prediction and disclosure clusters judge faithfulness from inputs and outputs, and [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) ("behavioural faithfulness") and [Malladi & Srivastava 2026](./#p=malladi2026directly) ("behavioral faithfulness") name their notions after this. The mechanism cluster and the self-consistency critics deny that such tests can settle the question. For [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) and [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) any such test "actually measures self-consistency rather than faithfulness". [Anonymous 2026, *GRACE*](./#p=anon2026grace) write that they "cannot separate a model whose answer is supported by a different mechanism than its CoT suggests from one whose answer is simply robust to perturbation". [Jia & Mu 2026](./#p=jia2026decorative) call load-bearingness "distinct from full mechanistic faithfulness". [Atkinson et al. 2026](./#p=atkinson2026identifying) make the split explicit for self-reports: a "correct confabulator" is faithful in the behavioural sense but not grounded. [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) add the converse case: "CoT text can causally impact behavior even if it does not straightforwardly “explain” model internals."

6. **May correctness enter the definition?** Several agreement-cluster definitions tie faithfulness to correct reasoning or answers. [Xu et al. 2024](./#p=xu2024faithful): "’unfaithful’ if the answer is correct but the process is not". [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making): "how reliably the model uses its reasoning steps to arrive at a correct answer". [Wang et al. 2025](./#p=wang2025enhancing): "unfaithful trajectories that guess the correct option after a faulty chain of thought". [Li et al. 2024](./#p=li2025better) compare the CoT with a gold rationale, and [Mohammadi et al. 2025](./#p=mohammadi2025evaluating) check whether the reasoning "logically entails the reference explanation". Other papers exclude correctness: "the evaluation of faithfulness should not involve human-provided gold labels" ([Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can), after [Jacovi & Goldberg 2020](./#p=jacovi2020towards)), and the two outputs "should output the same value for any strategy $S$ , regardless of whether the answer is correct, since CIA measures faithfulness rather than correctness" ([Hong et al. 2026](./#p=hong2026making)). On the stricter reading, a trace that accurately reports flawed reasoning is faithful, while the correctness-based definitions count it as unfaithful whenever the answer happens to be right.

## Counterfactual

The counterfactual family holds 292 definition rows from 87 of the 217 papers. By kind, 109 rows are operational, 84 informal, 45 formal, 29 criteria, 19 taxonomies and 6 failure modes. By stance, 160 are proposed, 86 adopted and 46 adapted. Of the 292 rows, 163 define a counterfactual notion (162 of them carry the word in the term) and 129 define a companion notion, such as a validity criterion, an effect measure or a simulation metric. The second pass over the full texts added 163 rows and 14 papers, and 113 of the added rows are companion notions. The rows fall into the 12 clusters below. In 10 of them the counterfactual is a variant (of the input, the reasoning, an internal state or the parameters) that the model is run with, and the output is compared with the output on the original. In cluster 1 the counterfactual explanation is defined by what an edited input "would lead" the model to do, and the edit is run only to check validity; in cluster 10 the counterfactual is training data or a reward signal. One row of cluster 12 runs no variant at all: in the counterfactual autorater of [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training), a judge asks whether the reasoning "could ... plausibly have been produced in a scenario where no defect existed". The clusters differ on four points: what is varied; whether the variant must change the output; how a change in output becomes a causal claim; and what the comparison judges (an explanation, the model's reliance on a feature, a self-report, a training target or a covert behavior).

In the tables, text in quotation marks is copied from the paper, with omissions marked by an ellipsis. Text without quotation marks is the extractor's gloss.

### 1. Counterfactual explanation: an edited input that would change the decision

This is the explainable-AI sense, which [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) and [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) take from Wachter et al. 2017. The counterfactual is an alternative input that would lead the model to a different decision, and that input is itself the explanation. In 5 of the 10 papers the model writes the edit itself, which gives a self-generated counterfactual explanation (SCE): [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self), [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can), [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms), [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) and [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating). The edit is judged by validity (whether the model's own prediction on the edited input reaches the target; [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) require a new context window), minimality and closeness. [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) separate validity from correctness, which asks whether the edit "actually evaluates to" the target by human judgment. Three papers make validity the condition for a trustworthy explanation: [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) ("If satisfied, the counterfactual is faithful"), [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) ("faithfulness can be evaluated by checking whether the generated counterfactuals indeed produce the prediction change") and [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) ("Without validity, the explanation is a misleading representation of the model’s counterfactual behaviour"). Three rows use "counterfactual explanation" for a statement about how behavior would change, not for the edited input: [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness), [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) and [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency). 28 rows from 10 papers (12 proposed, 13 adopted, 3 adapted).

> A counterfactual explanation $\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}}$ is a modified version of the original input $\mathbf{x}$ that would lead the model to change its decision, that is, $f(\mathbf{x})\neq f(\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}})$ .
>
> ([Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can), §3.1 (Step 2))

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) | counterfactual explanation | formal | “a modified version of the original input $\mathbf{x}$ that would lead the model to change its decision” |
| [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) | counterfactual explanation | informal | “an alternative input $x^{\prime}\neq x$ that would lead to a specific alternative output $y^{\prime}\neq y$” |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) | counterfactual explanation | informal | “Counterfactual explanations replace content, to get the opposite prediction” |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) | faithful counterfactual | criterion | “we can re-evaluate the prediction with the counterfactual and check that this goal is satisfied. If satisfied, the counterfactual is faithful.” |
| [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) | self-generated counterfactual explanation (SCE) | informal | “we do not solve an optimization problem and instead ask the model itself to generate the counterfactual explanation” |
| [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) | counterfactual validity (Val) | operational | “the percentage of times the SCE actually produces the intended target label” |
| [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) | SCE correctness | operational | “whether the counterfactual $\mathbf{x}_{CE}$ actually evaluates to $\hat{y}_{CE}$” |
| [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) | validity (of SCEs) | criterion | “the revised input should change the model’s prediction when re-evaluated in a new context window” |
| [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) | minimality (of SCEs) | criterion | “they should make the smallest edit required to change the outcome” |
| [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) | necessary criteria for valid and minimal SCEs | criterion | “(iii) they must be able to accurately self-predict their behaviour” |
| [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) | counterfactual-style self-explanation | informal | “faithfulness can be evaluated by checking whether the generated counterfactuals indeed produce the prediction change” |
| [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) | counterfactual pseudo-ground truth | operational | “replacing w∗ with another word wȳ associated with the second most probable prediction ȳ” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | self-generated counterfactual explanation | informal | “the model edits its own input until the prediction flips” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | Edit-Proposal (E8) | operational | “the example counts as flipped if the fraction of resamples that differ from baseline exceeds $0.5$” |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | counterfactual explanations (self-explanation type) | informal | “how different inputs might lead to different LLM responses” |
| [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) | counterfactual explanation | informal | “how behaviors change under an edit to its input (e.g. removal of a hint)” |
| [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency) | counterfactual explanation (self-description) | informal | “explain the input difference that induced the change or behavioral change itself” |

and 11 more: [Cahlik et al. 2025](./#p=cahlik2025reasoninggrounded) (counterfactual examples); [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) (counterfactual examples (explanations)); [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) (minimality of counterfactual explanations); [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) (edit distance (ED) of SCEs); [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) (generation percentage (Gen)); [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) (counterfactual-style self-explanation faithfulness); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) (self-generated counterfactual explanation (SCE)); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) (minimal counterfactual); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) (Validity (Val)); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) (Excess Distance (ED)); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) (Exact Match (EM)).

Formal versions:
- Counterfactual condition: $f(\mathbf{x})\neq f(\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}})$, with a chosen target $f(\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}})=y_{\text{CE}}$ when the counterfactual is generated ([Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can)).
- Validity: the share of instances with $f(\mathbf{x}_{\text{CE}})=y_{\text{CE}}$, computed without ($\mathrm{Val}$) and with ($\mathrm{Val}_C$) the earlier turns in context ([Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can)); [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) require the flip in a new context window.
- Minimal counterfactual ([Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms)): $z_{i,\text{M}}\in\arg\min_{z_i'\in\mathcal{Z}} d(z_i,z_i')$ subject to $f(\phi(z_i'))=y'$, with $d$ Gower's distance; excess distance $\mathrm{ED}_i=d(z_i,z_i')-d(z_i,z_{i,\text{M}})$ over valid SCEs, and Exact Match the share of instances whose SCE is a minimal counterfactual.
- Edit-Proposal ([Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating)): the model's edit counts as a flip when more than half of $K$ resamples on the edited prompt differ from the baseline answer; no target label is given.

### 2. Counterfactual simulatability: an explanation that predicts behavior on other inputs

Here the counterfactual is the test input, not the explanation. An explanation given on $x$ is judged by whether an observer who reads it predicts the model's output on other inputs $x'$. [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) define the notion for free-text explanations and give two metrics, precision and generality, both computed over the counterfactuals the observer judges simulatable. The observer is a human in Chen et al.'s definition (approximated by GPT-4), "a human or model" in [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching), LLM judges and a human study in [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm), and a simulator model in [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual). What the observer predicts also varies: the model's answer ([Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl)), whether a new request is refused or complied with ([Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl)), or whether a named prompt edit moves a behavior rate ([Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would)). [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) add a phase without the explanation and score the difference. The rows disagree on whether the notion measures faithfulness, plausibility or usefulness (Tensions, item 7). 34 rows from 15 papers (12 proposed, 14 adopted, 8 adapted).

> we propose to evaluate counterfactual simulatability of natural language explanations: whether an explanation can enable humans to precisely infer the model’s outputs on diverse counterfactuals of the explained input.
>
> ([Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl), Abstract)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “whether an explanation can enable humans to precisely infer the model’s outputs on diverse counterfactuals of the explained input” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | mental model / simulation | formal | “$h_{x,e_{x},o_{x}}(x^{\prime})$ denotes what the human infers to be $M$ ’s output on $x^{\prime}$ (simulation)” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | simulation precision | formal | “the fraction of simulatable counterfactuals where humans’ simulation matches the model’s actual output” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | simulation generality | formal | “one minus the average similarity between two simulatable counterfactuals” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | wrong mental model (misleading explanation) | failure-mode | “humans form a wrong mental model of GPT-4 (i.e., incorrectly infer how GPT-4 answers relevant counterfactuals)” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “whether model explanations on one input help humans predict what predictions models will give on other inputs” |
| [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) | counterfactual simulatability notion of faithfulness | criterion | “an explanation is faithful if and only if the explanation allows a human or model to accurately predict how the model will behave on other relevant inputs” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “the extent to which a model’s explanation on a certain input allows a user to predict the model’s answer for a different input” |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “rewarding CoTs that allow for a simulator model to predict a model’s answer on a counterfactual input” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “This tests whether an observer can predict a model’s behavior given its explanation.” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | counterfactual simulatability | informal | “an explanation is considered useful if it helps people anticipate how a model’s prediction would change on counterfactual inputs” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | counterfactual simulatability (acc_improvement) | formal | “comparing Phase 2 (with explanation) against Phase 1 (without explanation)” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | in-context bias toward the original answer (anchoring) | formal | “the judge’s prediction matches the original answer” |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) | explanation (counterfactual-testable behavioral claim) | informal | “a behavioral claim testable by counterfactual experiments, not an explanation of the model’s internal computation” |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) | counterfactual claim | operational | “a specific prompt edit changes how often a specified behavior occurs by at least 30 percentage points” |
| [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl) | counterfactual refusal and compliance accuracy | operational | “the fraction of examples where responses match the predicted behavior” |
| [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness) | counterfactual simulatability | operational | Restates Chen et al.’s simulation generality and simulation precision. |

and 17 more: [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) (counterfactual simulatability precision (explanation consistency)); [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) (simulated human expectation (follow-up answer from the explanation)); [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) (counterfactual simulation); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (LLM-as-a-judge counterfactual simulation); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (simulation accuracy (guess_rate, selective accuracy, overall accuracy)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (human simulation user study (two-phase)); [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) (counterfactual simulatability); [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) (counterfactual simulatability test (tool uplift)); [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal) (counterfactual inputs (counterfactual simulation training)); [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) (predicting LLM behavior on counterfactual questions (counterfactual simulatability)); [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) (counterfactual inputs from explanations (logical predicates)); [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) (generalizable and precise explanation (desiderata)); [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) (human simulation as logical entailment); [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) (LLM simulator (simulation prompt)); [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) (contrastive simulation); [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl) (counterfactual simulatability evaluation); [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl) (predictive view of explanation (counterfactual inputs)).

Formal versions ([Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) unless noted):
- Mental model: $h_{x,e_x,o_x}:X\to O\cup\{\perp\}$, where $h(x')=\perp$ marks $x'$ as unsimulatable.
- Simulation precision: $\frac{1}{|C^*|}\sum_{x'\in C^*}\mathbf{1}[h_{e_x}(x')=o_{x'}]$ over the simulatable counterfactuals $C^*$.
- Simulation generality: $1-\mathbb{E}_{x',x''\sim p}[\alpha(x',x'')]$, with $\alpha$ a similarity metric over simulatable counterfactuals.
- Gain over a no-explanation phase ([Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm)): $\Delta\mathrm{OverallAcc}=\mathrm{OverallAcc}^{\text{P2}}-\mathrm{OverallAcc}^{\text{P1}}$, with cannot_guess counted as incorrect.
- Anchoring of the simulator ([Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm)): $\widehat{\mathrm{bias}}^{\text{orig}}(p)=\frac{1}{N_p}\sum_{i:\,p_i=p}B_i$, with $B_i=\mathbb{I}[\hat{y}_i=a_i]$.
- Counterfactual claim label ([Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would)): true if the edit moved the behavior rate by at least 50 percentage points, false if by at most 15.

### 3. The counterfactual set: which inputs simulatability is tested on

These rows define the inputs $x'$ on which an explanation from cluster 2 is tested. Six papers give five constructions:
- follow-up questions that an LLM writes from the explanation, instructed to write one "that you can confidently guess the robot’s answer to" ([Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl)), extended with exact paraphrases by [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) and used as the "non-pragmatic baseline" by [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm);
- the input with a planted cue removed ([Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual));
- LLM-generated related questions, rejection-sampled so that "the task model and simulator disagree on the correct answer" ([Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual));
- four Gricean manipulations: presupposition flip, lexical substitution, scalar adjustment and contextualization ([Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm));
- real dataset rows close to the original in Hamming distance ([Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm), [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient)).

[Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) state that "the validity of any evaluation hinges on this choice", restate three earlier designs as counterfactual sets (a biased prompt, random word insertion, single-concept edits) and name three pitfalls. [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) concede that "LLMs most likely do not cover all simulatable counterfactuals". 26 rows from 6 papers (15 proposed, 6 adopted, 5 adapted).

> For a question $x$ , we construct a set of counterfactuals $C(x)$ : inputs that are similar to $x$ , but differ in some way, such that a faithful explanation of the reference model’s behavior on $x$ should help predict its behavior across $C(x)$ .
>
> ([Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm), §2.2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | counterfactual (distribution) | informal | “any simulatable counterfactual in the natural language distribution” |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | counterfactual generation (LLM prompt) | operational | “write a follow-up yes or no question that you can confidently guess the robot’s answer to based on its answer to the starter question” |
| [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) | follow-up questions (related vs. rephrased) | taxonomy | “related questions and rephrased questions” |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | cue-based counterfactual | informal | “The counterfactual input is the input without the cue.” |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | model-based counterfactual | informal | “generate diverse, generic counterfactuals (our model-based counterfactuals) that test a model’s understanding of a topic by asking closely related questions” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | natural language counterfactuals | informal | “minimal what-if variations of an input” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | pragmatic counterfactual taxonomy | taxonomy | “Each counterfactual corresponds to a controlled manipulation of one or more maxims.” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | counterfactual (edited input x̂) | informal | “manually edit test examples in small but meaningful ways that usually flip the gold label” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | data-driven counterfactuals | operational | “quantifying closeness with Hamming distance: the number of dataset features that differ between two inputs” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | too little perturbation | failure-mode | “Most prior approaches vary only a single concept at a time” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | irrelevant or incoherent perturbations | failure-mode | “often produce counterfactuals that are irrelevant or drift from the natural data distribution” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | testing external knowledge | failure-mode | “predicting the reference model’s behavior requires inferring its world knowledge rather than purely applying its stated reasoning” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | biased-prompt test as a single-element counterfactual set | operational | “testing some point $x_{\text{no bias}}$ with the counterfactual set $C(x)=\{x_{\text{bias}}\}$” |
| [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) | counterfactual pair (Moral Machines) | operational | “share the same set of character types and binary flags but differ in the counts of those characters” |
| [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient) | counterfactual input (similar real input) | informal | “similar real inputs from the same dataset rather than synthetic perturbations” |

and 11 more: [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) (counterfactual input (cue-based vs model-based)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (counterfactual reasoning); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (LLM automatic counterfactual generation); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (presupposition flip (counterfactual type)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (lexical substitution (counterfactual type)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (scalar adjustment (counterfactual type)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (contextualization (counterfactual type)); [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (counterfactual set C(x)); [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (pitfalls in choosing the counterfactual set); [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (random word-insertion counterfactuals); [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (concept space perturbation).

Formal versions ([Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm)):
- Data-driven set: $C(x)$ holds dataset rows within Hamming distance $\le 2$ of $x$, balanced by label.
- A cue test as a set with one element: $C(x)=\{x_{\text{bias}}\}$ for the point $x_{\text{no bias}}$.
- Word insertion: $C(x)$ holds $x_w^{(i)}$, the input with word $w$ inserted at sampled position $i\in\{1,\dots,m\}$.

### 4. Counterfactual self-prediction: the model reports how it would behave under an edit

In this cluster the observer of cluster 2 is the model itself. It is asked whether, or how much, an edit would change its own behavior, and its answer is scored against runs on the edited input. [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) call this self-modeling, "an LLM’s ability to answer questions about its own behavior", and test it with a benchmark of tasks, five of which are rows here (Flip-Decision, Flip-Rate, Perturbation-Choice, Component-Attribution and Score-Prediction); [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) train it as "causal self-report"; [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) train an observer role in the same weights and reward it with a Brier score. [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) score factor-importance self-reports against counterfactual audits and show that the audit rule decides whether truthful reporting pays: "report-dependent auditing creates a suppression incentive". [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) name self-prediction as one of three necessary criteria for valid and minimal self-generated counterfactuals (cluster 1). A claim the model makes about its own behavior is scored as true or false against runs on the edited input, which makes this cluster the counterfactual counterpart of the honesty and self-report rows in the other two families. 11 rows from 5 papers (6 proposed, 1 adopted, 4 adapted).

> In Flip-Decision, we test whether a model can predict whether a prompt perturbation will change its own aggregate behavior.
>
> ([Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating), §2.4.1)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | counterfactual question (Flip-Decision) | operational | “whether a model can predict whether a prompt perturbation will change its own aggregate behavior” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | Flip-Rate (E3) | operational | “How likely is this perturbation to flip your output?” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | Perturbation-Choice (E6) | operational | “The behavioral ground truth label is the option with the highest flip rate.” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | Component-Attribution (E7) | operational | “remove each component in turn and measure how often the model’s sampled answer changes relative to its baseline behavior” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | Score-Prediction (BLOOM self-modeling task) | operational | “predict the post-perturbation judge score without observing the full original or perturbed trajectory” |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) | counterfactual prediction (causal self-report) | operational | “posed as a follow-up turn on its own transcript, the model is shown one counterfactual claim” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) | counterfactual prediction | informal | “questions about how the assistant’s behavior would change with a specific edit to the input” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) | counterfactual prediction reward (Brier) | formal | “the reward was defined as 1−(p−y)2 , where p is the model prediction and y is the target” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight) | forward vs. reverse counterfactual items | taxonomy | “needs to predict the reverse counterfactual” |
| [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) | Counterfactual Brier Score (CBS) | operational | “a coordinate-wise Brier score applied to counterfactual audit outcomes” |
| [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) | self-prediction | informal | “models are attempting to predict their own behaviour, i.e. self-prediction” |

Formal versions:
- Observer reward ([Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight)): $1-(p-y)^2$, where $y=(k+\tfrac12)/(n+1)$ is the smoothed share of $n=10$ assistant samples showing the behavior.
- Counterfactual Brier score ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit)): $S=-\sum_j(p_j-\hat{D}_j)^2$ between reported factor importances $p_j$ and counterfactual flip fractions $\hat{D}_j$ (cluster 6); strictly proper under exogenous verification "when the verification estimates are unbiased".

### 5. Input intervention: the same input with one cue or factor changed

This is the largest cluster by papers. A counterfactual is the original input with one specified element removed, inserted or replaced, with everything else held fixed, and a difference between the two outputs is read as that element's influence. The element is one of seven kinds:
- a planted hint, cue or biasing feature ([Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek), [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring), [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability), [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length), [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient), [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training), [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness), [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would), [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective), [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary), [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain), [Li 2026](./#p=li2026monitorability));
- a concept or structured factor ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk), [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal), [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases), [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit));
- a protected attribute or user background ([Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful), [Pres et al. 2026a](./#p=pres2026consistency));
- the evidence passage ([Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when));
- an earlier turn of a conversation ([Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating));
- a note tying a valued outcome to the answer ([Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight));
- an edit that changes the correct answer ([Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal), [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case)).

The rows cite three traditions for the design. [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) call it "standard in the bias and fairness literature", [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) take it from Kaushik et al. 2019, and [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) note that it has "been widely used to assess chain-of-thought faithfulness" and monitorability. [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) state the edit with Pearl's do-operator as a direct effect. 45 rows from 30 papers (19 proposed, 15 adopted, 11 adapted).

> Let $\mathbf{x}_{c_{m}\rightarrow c_{m}^{\prime}}$ denote the counterfactual input that results from an intervention that changes the concept $C_{m}$ from $c_{m}$ to $c_{m}^{\prime}$ but keeps all other aspects of the question $\mathbf{x}$ (including the values of all other concepts) the same.
>
> ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk), §2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | counterfactual input (concept intervention) | formal | “changes the concept $C_{m}$ from $c_{m}$ to $c_{m}^{\prime}$ but keeps all other aspects of the question $\mathbf{x}$ (including the values of all other concepts) the same” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | removal-based counterfactual | operational | “edit $\mathbf{x}$ so that the value of a concept $C_{m}$ cannot be determined” |
| [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) | counterfactual replacement (perturbed input) | formal | “The perturbed input $x^{\setminus j,v}$ is identical to $x$ except that factor $j$ is replaced by $v$ .” |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | two types of counterfactuals | taxonomy | “model predictions are insensitive to features not referenced by explanations … model predictions are sensitive to features that are relied on by their explanations” |
| [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | cue (prompt insertion) | informal | “an insertion into the question prompt that points to a particular response” |
| [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) | cue-ablated input | formal | “$x_{\setminus C}$ for the cue-ablated input obtained by removing $C$ from $x$” |
| [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) | intervention-based evaluation (input with vs. without intervention) | informal | “compare the prediction of a model when given an input with ( $X=1$ ) and without ( $X=0$ ) some intervention applied to it” |
| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | matched or counterfactual inputs (intervention vs. control arm) | informal | “Evaluations that compare how a model’s behavior shifts across matched or counterfactual inputs are standard in the bias and fairness literature” |
| [Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) | counterfactual test of cue reliance | criterion | “evidence that the protected feature (which was omitted when producing $y^{\prime}$ ) exerts causal influence on $\operatorname{pred}(y)$” |
| [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) | nearby counterfactual (the hint caused the answer) | criterion | “Comparing W to a no-hint prompt would confound the hint’s content with its presence” |
| [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) | counterfactual (hint-induced answer flip) | formal | “If the model’s new answer with the hint $a_{1}$ differs from the original $a_{0}$ ( $a_{0}\neq a_{1}=hint$ ), we regard this as a counterfactual” |
| [Li 2026](./#p=li2026monitorability) | counterfactual hint effect | criterion | “per-problem no-hint / correct-hint / incorrect-hint generations defining influence from the counterfactual answer shift” |
| [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) | fork (counterfactual conversation variant) | informal | “rewind to a previous turn, modify one element, replay to completion” |

| [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | input contrast sets (contrast consistency / spurious invariance) | operational | “the model correctly changes its answer when the input contrast requires it” |
