[Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (fork types (multi-turn counterfactual edits)).

Formal versions:
- Factor replacement: $x^{\setminus j,v}$ equals $x$ except that factor $j$ takes the value $v\sim\mathcal{P}_j$ ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit)).
- Cue ablation: $x_{\setminus C}$ ([Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective)); input ablation $T_{\text{input}}(\mathcal{M},x,\tilde{x})=d(\mathcal{M}(x),\mathcal{M}(x\backslash\tilde{x}))$ ([Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training)).
- Insertion: $x'=x\oplus\Delta$ ([Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training)).
- Paired concept variants $x^{+}_{c}$ (promoting $c$) and $x^{-}_{c}$ (diminishing $c$) ([Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases)).
- Direct effect of a concept ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk)): $\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y\mid do(C_m=c_m',\{C_i=c_i\}_{i\neq m},V=v)]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[Y\mid do(\{C_i=c_i\}_{i\in 1,\ldots,M},V=v)]$.
- Hint flip: $a_0\neq a_1=\text{hint}$ ([Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness)).

### 6. From paired runs to a causal claim: flip labels, total effects and potential outcomes

These rows state how runs on an original and a variant become a claim that the edit caused the outcome. They give four answers, from the most to the least per-instance:
- a flip label on one pair ([Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary)), or a flip fraction over $n=3$ sampled replacements per factor by default ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit));
- a resampled rate with thresholds ([Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would): positive when the cue raises the rate by at least 50 points, negative when it moves the rate by at most 15; [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating): three bands of flip fraction);
- a population effect: the total effect ([Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain), [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought)), the hint-caused switch rate ([Little 2026](./#p=little2026length)), the KL-based concept effect ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk)) or the gap between paired variants ([Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases));
- bounds on potential outcomes in a structural causal model ([Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving)).

Three papers explain why a single flip is not enough. [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) reject the per-response criterion because it "ignores the stochastic and chaotic nature of LLM output sampling". [Angdembay et al. 2026](./#p=angdembay2026two) note that uncoupled sampling records "an observed conditional flip rather than a within-pair counterfactual". [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) show that separate arm samples do not identify "this cross-world coupling". [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) find that a third alternate candidate "flips 11-23% of verdicts", so their dependence label depends on which alternate defines the counterfactual. 23 rows from 13 papers (17 proposed, 2 adopted, 4 adapted).

> Consider the rate at which the model predicts the answer that matches the hint when the hint is present, versus when it is absent; we term the difference in these two rates the total effect, $\mathrm{TE}$ .
>
> ([Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), §4)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) | total effect (TE) of the hint | operational | “we term the difference in these two rates the total effect” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought) | total effect (hint intervention vs. control arm) | formal | “Total effect is P (y = 1\|x = 1) - P (y = 1\|x = 0).” |
| [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) | total effect (attribute removal) | formal | “a lower bound on the rate of target-answer responses with systematic attribute influence” |
| [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) | per-response flip criterion (rejected) | criterion | “This criterion, however, ignores the stochastic and chaotic nature of LLM output sampling.” |
| [Angdembay et al. 2026](./#p=angdembay2026two) | within-pair counterfactual (vs. observed conditional flip) | criterion | “the label records an observed conditional flip rather than a within-pair counterfactual” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) | counterfactual target (intervention-caused outcome) | informal | “detecting intervention-caused outcomes is a counterfactual target” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) | potential outcome and monitor decision under do(X = x) | formal | “Let Y (x) and Z(x) denote the potential outcome and monitor decision under do(X = x)” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) | cross-world coupling (non-identification of causal recall) | criterion | “Separate arm samples identify the arm distributions but generally not this cross-world coupling.” |
| [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) | single-factor intervention (identification assumption) | criterion | “$\Delta$ is the only controlled difference between $X$ and $X^{\prime}$ , so any change in behavior can be attributed to it” |
| [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) | hint-caused switch rate (hint influence) under uniform switching | formal | “$\alpha=I/p$ is the fraction of switches to $h$ that the hint caused” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | causal concept effect (CE) | formal | “The Kullback-Leibler divergence between $\mathcal{M}$ ’s answer distribution in response to counterfactual input” |
| [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) | same-answer counterfactual (dependence label) | operational | “a trace is dependent if the model does not answer h under the matched control and independent if it does” |
| [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) | ambiguous example (verdict depends on the alternate candidate) | criterion | “the dependence verdict flips depending on which alternate wrong candidate defines the counterfactual” |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) | cue-influence label (hint setting) | operational | “positive cases are those in which the cue raises the rate of the cue-consistent answer by at least 50pp” |
| [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) | empirical influence estimate (counterfactual flip fraction) | formal | Share of $n$ sampled replacements of factor $j$ that change the answer ($n=3$ by default). |

and 8 more: [Anonymous 2026 (causal bounds)](./#p=anon2026improving) (Assumption 1 (one SCM, consistency)); [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) (effect size Δ (bias strength)); [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) (direct effect on the early answer (frozen CoT state)); [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) (pair-centered causal effect (role-swapped benchmark)); [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (empirical flip rate); [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (flip (label mode vs. feature mode)); [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (perturbation categories (flip_inducing / boundary / non_flip)); [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) (score-changing perturbation (BLOOM flip)).

Formal versions:
- Total effect: $P(y=1\mid x=1)-P(y=1\mid x=0)$ ([Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought)); $\mathrm{TE}:=p_{11}-p_{01}$, a lower bound on systematically influenced responses when $\mathrm{TE}\ge 0$ ([Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain)).
- Hint-caused switch rate under uniform switching: $I=p-q/(\bar{n}-2)$ and $\alpha=I/p$ ([Little 2026](./#p=little2026length)).
- Causal concept effect: $\mathrm{CE}(\mathbf{x},C_m)=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{C}'_m|}\sum_{c'_m}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(Y\mid\mathbf{x}_{c_m\to c'_m})\,\|\,\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{M}}(Y\mid\mathbf{x})\big)$ ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk)).
- Empirical flip rate: $g(x)=\frac{1}{K^2}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\mathbf{1}[a_b^{(i)}(x)\neq a_\ell^{(j)}(x)]$ ([Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating)).
- Influence estimate: $\hat{D}_j=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}[M(x^{\setminus j,v_i})\neq y]$ ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit)).
- Bias strength: $\Delta=p_{\text{pos}}-p_{\text{neg}}$ ([Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases)).
- Direct effect on the early answer with the CoT state frozen: $\mathrm{DE}_{\mathrm{EA}}^{(i^*)}=P(Y=1\mid X=1,do(H^{(i^*)}=H_0^{(i^*)}))-P(Y=1\mid X=0)$ ([Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain)).
- Pair-centered effect: $\log p(h_1\mid W(h_1))-\log p(h_1\mid G(h_2))$ minus the pair mean ([Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary)).

### 7. Valid counterfactuals: isolation, quality and controls

These rows state what a variant must satisfy before an output change can be attributed to the intended edit. Thirteen papers give seven kinds of requirement:
- isolation: only the target changes ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit), [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk), [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases), [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would));
- quality of a generated edit: coherence and completeness ([Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk)), four criteria for injected reasoning ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval)), semantic opposition of a contrary hint ([Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm)), label flip rate and textual similarity ([Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm)), and residual leakage of the original answer ([Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when));
- on-policy edits: text "the model could have plausibly produced" ([Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought));
- informativeness: the injected reasoning must oppose the model's baseline stance ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval));
- executability: both plans must succeed under forced execution ([Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan));
- controls: semantics-preserving null edits ([Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal)) and matched swaps that keep the claim fixed ([Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable));
- for a parameter edit, efficacy and specificity of unlearning ([Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring)).

Two papers report how much their checks remove: the isolation check of [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) "removed $42$ % of candidate concepts", and [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) "started with 8,499 instances and removed 1,313". 25 rows from 13 papers (22 proposed, 2 adopted, 1 adapted).

> Good perturbations should preserve the coherence of the input, vary the target factor enough to change the answer when that factor matters, and leave the other factors fixed.
>
> ([Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit), §3.2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Zhang et al. 2026](./#p=zhang2026audit) | counterfactual distribution (perturbation design) | criterion | “vary the target factor enough to change the answer when that factor matters, and leave the other factors fixed” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) | confound (in counterfactual variations) | failure-mode | “changes to the input that are unrelated to the target concept but could independently affect the model’s decision” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) | not a valid counterfactual (variation rating 1) | taxonomy | “Multiple or major changes; cannot attribute differences to the concept.” |
| [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) | confounded / unstable counterfactual | criterion | “A counterfactual may be confounded, editing several variables at once.” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | disentangled concepts (assumption for concept interventions) | criterion | “each concept $C_{m}$ can be changed without affecting other concepts $C_{n\neq m}$” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | counterfactual quality (coherency, completeness, minimality) | criterion | “Coherency: Is the question grammatically correct and semantically coherent?” |
| [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) | on-policy intervention (vs. off-policy) | informal | “We call an intervention on-policy if the model could have plausibly produced the new text from the upstream CoT.” |
| [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) | proper counterfactual contrast (contrast precondition) | criterion | “For causal identifiability, we impose a contrast precondition” |
| [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) | valid counterfactual reasoning (validation rubric) | criterion | “the reasoning is sufficient to steer a reader toward exactly one specific incorrect answer” |
| [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | accepted causally-invalidating edit | formal | “accepted by the verifier as breaking the trace validity” |
| [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | null interventions (answer preservation / spurious flip rate) | operational | “semantics-preserving transformations that should not change the answer” |
| [Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable) | matched counterfactual (claim fixed, evidence swapped) | operational | “holding the claim text fixed while swapping only the evidence passage” |
| [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) | forced execution (validity gate) | criterion | “behavioral proof that both outcomes are achievable rather than merely plausible” |
| [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | valid intervention (efficacy and specificity controls) | criterion | “the target information is removed (high efficacy), but the model retains its general capabilities” |
| [Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when) | residual leakage (counterfactual evidence) | failure-mode | “an edited version can still imply $a_{0}$ in ways that the string checks do not catch” |
| [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) | label flip rate | operational | “counterfactuals that preserve the surface form of $Q$ but flip the label are considered more challenging and informative” |

and 9 more: [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) (valid counterfactual (variation isolation check)); [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) (clean isolation (variation rating 5)); [Chuang et al. 2024](./#p=chuang2026faithlm) (contrary-hint quality (semantic opposition check)); [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) (validity gates (ratification, forced execution, success-only scoring)); [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) (success-only scoring (validity gate)); [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) (contrast coverage c(M)); [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) (externality penalty E(r′) (locality of counterfactual reasoning)); [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) (minimal counterfactual (textual similarity)); [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) (verified explanation (counterfactual support score)).

Formal versions:
- Contrast precondition and coverage ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval)): $\delta(x,r';\mathcal{M})=\mathbf{1}\{S(r)\neq S(r')\}$ and $c(\mathcal{M})=\Pr_{(x,r')\sim\mathcal{D}}(\delta(x,r';\mathcal{M})=1)$.
- Externality penalty ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval)): $E(r')=1-\mathrm{Jaccard}(\mathcal{V}_x,\mathcal{V}_{r'})$.
- Accepted edits ([Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal)): $q\triangleq\Pr(A)$ and $\mu_A\triangleq\mathbb{E}[D(p(Y\mid X,T),p(Y\mid X,T'))\mid A]$, where $A$ is the event that the edit is proposed and accepted as breaking trace validity.

### 8. The counterfactual test of explanations: does the explanation mention the edit?

These rows use the same counterfactual as cluster 5, an input edit, but they judge the explanation, not the model's reliance on the edited element. The procedure is that of Atanasova et al. 2023, which 13 of the 17 rows cite: words are inserted into the input, and if the prediction changes while the explanation of the edited instance does not mention the inserted words, the explanation counts as unfaithful. Siegel et al. 2024, cited by 8 rows, replace the binary outcome with a correlation between mentions and the shift in the label distribution (the Correlational Counterfactual Test, CCT). Neither paper is in the map. Variants replace the inserted words with GPT-4o-generated modifiers ([Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique)) or with hint keywords ([Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness)), and [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) describe the family as methods that generate counterfactual texts with auxiliary models. Two rows are proposed, both by [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique): the counter rate and the use of the inserted word as "the true reasoning factor". 17 rows from 12 papers (2 proposed, 12 adopted, 3 adapted).

> The counterfactual test works by making an intervention to the original instance to get an intervened instance. The evaluation then consists of two steps: (1) Identify counter instances: intervened instances whose prediction changes compared to the original instance. (2) Identify unfaithful instances: counter instances whose NLEs (generated by baseline methods or SR-NLE) do not contain the intervened word (determined by string matching).
>
> ([Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique), §4.3)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) | Counterfactual Edits (test) | operational | “If the LLM changes its prediction after the counterfactual intervention, and the explanation does not mention the inserted words, the explanation is judged unfaithful” |
| [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) | counterfactual test | operational | “counter instances whose NLEs (generated by baseline methods or SR-NLE) do not contain the intervened word (determined by string matching)” |
| [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) | intervention (inserted adjective/adverb) | operational | “For nouns, we prepend a random adjective, and for verbs, we prepend a random adverb.” |
| [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) | intervened word as the true reasoning factor | criterion | “the intervened word (i.e., the true reasoning factor that made the label change)” |
| [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) | counterfactual edit (faithfulness test) | operational | “insert tokens that flip the prediction and check whether explanations reflect these edits” |
| [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) | Counterfactual Edits (faithfulness metric) | operational | “assess changes in explanations resulting from input modifications” |
| [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) | Counterfactual Tests (CT) (original) | operational | “making small edits to the input, typically only a few tokens, and then checking whether those tokens appear in the explanation” |
| [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) | Counterfactual Test (CT) | operational | “verifying whether the explanation explicitly mentions a small set of distinctive keywords associated with each hint type” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2026](./#p=arcuschin2026biases) | Correlational Counterfactual Test (CCT) | operational | “considers the total shift in predicted label distributions rather than just binary outcomes” |
| [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) | CCT (correlational counterfactual test) | informal | “computes population-level correlations between counterfactual interventions and explanation mentions” |
| [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) | CCT / Phi-CCT (correlational counterfactual measure) | informal | “refined this into a correlational measure, aligning mentions with the graded influence of interventions” |
| [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) | Counterfactual Intervention (CI) faithfulness test | operational | “The self-NLE is deemed faithful if it aligns with the specific CI that caused the prediction change.” |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | counterfactual edits (faithfulness test) | operational | “they assess the faithfulness of explanations given to the counterfactual questions” |
| [Gema et al. 2026](./#p=gema2026chainofthought) | counterfactual approaches (explanation faithfulness tests) | informal | “test whether free-text explanations reflect model decisions through input edits, reconstruction, or output-distribution shifts” |
| [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift) | counterfactual generation (perturbed input) | informal | “we modify key parts w of the inputs I and examine the resulting changes in the generated rationales” |

and 2 more: [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) (counterfactual tests); [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) (counter rate).

Formal version, restated from [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) and [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique): let $x'$ be the edited input and $w$ the inserted words; an instance is unfaithful when $f(x')\neq f(x)$ and $w$ does not occur in the explanation $e(x')$. [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) check occurrence by string matching and report the counter rate, the share of 20,000 intervened instances per model and dataset whose prediction changes.

### 9. Intervention on the reasoning: an edited, truncated or resampled chain of thought

This is the largest cluster by rows. The counterfactual is a version of the model's own intermediate output with the input held fixed: the chain of thought in 41 of the 49 rows, and in the other 8 a generated mediator, a seeded plan, a rationale span or a stored trace crossed with edited evidence. Thirteen of the 49 rows cite [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring). The edits are of eight kinds:
- truncation and forced early answering ([Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop), [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace), [Wang et al. 2026c](./#p=wang2026verbalising), [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting));
- an inserted mistake or a single decisive error ([Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting), [Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp));
- paraphrase ([Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace), [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting));
- a contradicting step, operator swap, changed conclusion or irrelevant fact ([Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project), [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal), [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability), [Swaroop et al. 2025](./#p=swaroop2025lift));
- a substituted whole rationale or injected flawed reasoning ([Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc), [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval), [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith));
- resampling from a sentence onward, and transplanting a hinted prefix into the unhinted prompt ([Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought));
- counterfactual chains in a causal mediation analysis ([Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making), [Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms), [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept), [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought));
- edits to other intermediates: a generated mediator ([Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking)), a seeded plan ([Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan)), a rationale or evidence span ([Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable)), a stored trace answered against edited evidence ([Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when)).

If the answer follows the edit, it is taken to depend on the reasoning. [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) write that "Higher COS indicates greater faithfulness"; [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) describe such tests as ones that "implicitly treat faithfulness as a causal metric" and argue that for monitorability the relevant question is whether CoT tokens "explain the target variable", not whether they cause it (Tensions, item 9). 49 rows from 26 papers (27 proposed, 18 adopted, 4 adapted).

> Also, it should be constructed to admit localized output-level counterfactual edits to the reasoning trace while holding the input fixed, allowing attribution of ensuing output changes to the stated reasoning.
>
> ([Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval), §3.1)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) | output-level counterfactual reasoning intervention | informal | “localized output-level counterfactual edits to the reasoning trace while holding the input fixed” |
| [Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project) | counterfactual thought | formal | “$do(s_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k})$ , where $s^{\prime}_{k}$ is a counterfactual thought generated to contradict the original reasoning” |
| [Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project) | counterfactual answer | formal | “we generate a counterfactual answer $a^{*}$ by re-executing the agent from the point of intervention” |
| [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | Counterfactual Outcome Sensitivity (COS) | operational | “the percentage of correctly-answered questions where logical perturbations change the final answer” |
| [Cox et al. 2026](./#p=cox2026posthoc) | counterfactual rationale | informal | “a counterfactual rationale that entails the opposite label (substitution)” |
| [Swaroop et al. 2025](./#p=swaroop2025lift) | counterfactual intervention (irrelevant-fact step swap) | operational | “we replace $r_{i}$ with a domain-matched but irrelevant fact” |
| [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) | counterfactual draft | informal | “we alter the draft’s conclusion to create a counterfactual version $T^{\prime}$” |
| [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) | counterfactual resampling | operational | “resampling from position $i$ onward, keeping $S_{1},\ldots,S_{i-1}$ as prefix” |
| [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) | transplant resampling | operational | “transplant portions of said CoT into a model prompted with the same question but without any hint” |
| [Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop) | prefix counterfactual (forced answering) | formal | “append a stop-and-answer instruction to prefix $y_{1:t}$ and greedily decode an answer” |
| [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) | counterfactual operator Δ (counterfactual-based methods) | formal | “If $a^{\prime}\neq a$ , the perturbed reasoning content is considered causally influential to the prediction.” |
| [Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp) | targeted semantic perturbation c_err | operational | “contains exactly ONE decisive error introduced at a single reasoning step” |
| [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) | counterfactual reasoning (intervention on input or mediator) | informal | “measure their effect on the reasoning outcome $Y$ by keeping $R$ fixed (direct effect)” |
| [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) | counterfactual intervention (CNF) | informal | “A faithful model should shift its output accordingly.” |
| [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) | counterfactual plan seeding | criterion | “swaps between two genuinely valid, agent-endorsed plans” |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | intervention methods (causal faithfulness tests) | informal | “implicitly treat faithfulness as a causal metric by intervening on latent representations and observing changes in target variables” |

and 33 more: [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) (Counterfactual Sensitivity (CS)); [Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable) (counterfactual following (span swap)); [Ananthula & Kumarappan 2026](./#p=ananthula2026decodable) (counterfactual example (proof/rationale cross-wiring)); [Angdembay et al. 2026](./#p=angdembay2026two) (graph-targeted counterfactual interventions); [Anonymous 2026 (DCO)](./#p=anon2026generating) (counterfactual target (intervention contexts)); [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) (counterfactual perturbation regimes); [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) (paired graph experiment (perturbed CoT c′)); [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) (CoT perturbation (ablation, mistake insertion)); [Fareed 2026](./#p=fareed2026plan) (validity-gated counterfactual test); [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) (multi-turn counterfactual injection (closed-API approximation)); [Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project) (intervention modalities (counterfactual step types)); [Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms) (counterfactual CoT content); [Li et al. 2026](./#p=li2026when) (crossed trace-evidence conflicts (matched and crossed cells)); [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith) (step-level counterfactual verification); [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith) (counterfactual reasoning intervention (RFEval)); [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) (counterfactual importance); [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) (counterfactual++ importance); [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) (resilience); [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought) (sentence-cluster causal effect); [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) (mathematical counterfactual (edited CoT)); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (counterfactual necessity score); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (counterfactual sentence); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (counterfactual-based methods (perturbation strategies)); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (Adding Mistakes); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (Option Shuffling); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (Removing Steps); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (Early Answering); [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) (Paraphrasing); [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) (adversarial counterfactual sensitivity vs. constructive sensitivity); [Somov et al. 2026](./#p=somov2026breaking) (intervened mediator with deterministic counterfactual target); [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) (counterfactual reasoning-chain interventions); [Wang et al. 2026c](./#p=wang2026verbalising) (counterfactual-based baseline (Early Ans)); [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought) (counterfactual interventions on the CoT).

Formal versions:
- Counterfactual Sensitivity: $\mathrm{CS}(X;T\to T')=D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(p(Y\mid X,T)\,\|\,p(Y\mid X,T')\big)$; Counterfactual Outcome Sensitivity (COS) is its binary-flip version ([Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal)).
- Step intervention $do(s_k=s'_k)$, with counterfactual answer $a^*=f_a(q,\{s_1,\dots,\iota(s_k),\dots,s^*_n\};\theta)$, where later steps are re-sampled ([Khanzadeh 2026](./#p=khanzadeh2026project)).
- Counterfactual importance $D_{KL}[p(A'_{S_i}\mid T_i\not\approx S_i)\,\|\,p(A_{S_i})]$ and counterfactual++ importance $D_{KL}[p(A'_{S_i}\mid \forall j\ge i: T_j\not\approx S_i)\,\|\,p(A_{S_i})]$ ([Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought)).
- Sentence-cluster effect: $\mathrm{Effect}(C)=\mathbb{E}_{s_i\in C}[p(A_{\text{yes}}\mid\mathrm{CoT}_{1\dots i})-p(A_{\text{yes}}\mid\mathrm{CoT}_{1\dots i-1})]$ ([Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought)).
- Counterfactual operator: $\mathcal{C}'=\Delta(\mathcal{C})$ and $a'=\mathrm{M}_{\text{Ans}}(q,\mathcal{C}')$ ([Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting)); counterfactual sentence $r_t^{(\mu\to\tilde{o})}=(o_{t,<\mu},\tilde{o}_{t,\mu},\hat{o}_{t,>\mu})$ with the suffix regenerated ([Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting)).
- Paired graph experiment $E=(x,c,c',y)$, with attribution graphs $G_0=G(x,c,y)$ and $G_1=G(x,c',y)$ over one feature space ([Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace)).

### 10. Counterfactual training pairs

These rows use counterfactuals as training data or as a reward signal, not as a test. The counterfactual is a rationale or context, paired with the output the model should give on it. The training goes in one of two opposite directions:
- sensitivity: the model is trained to follow a rationale that leads to a different answer ([Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott), [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making), [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case), [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal)) or to lower its confidence when the rationale is masked or corrupted ([Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto));
- invariance: the model is trained to give, under a biasing or pressuring context, the response it gives without that context ([Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented), [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning)). [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) call the targets "the model’s own honest response" and add an acknowledgment of the pressure to the trace.

[Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) use the counterfactual for a reward model instead: a disclaimer of cue use is appended when the prediction without the cue differs. 15 rows from 9 papers (12 proposed, 3 adapted).

> we train the student to conduct counterfactual reasoning Roese (1997), i.e., answer accordingly when the rationale is leading to a different answer.
>
> ([Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott), §3.2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott) | counterfactual reasoning | informal | “answer accordingly when the rationale is leading to a different answer” |
| [Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott) | counterfactual rationale / counterfactual reasoning loss | formal | “a counterfactual rationale $r^{{}^{\prime}}$ that leads to the wrong answer $a^{{}^{\prime}}$” |
| [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) | counterfactual chains | informal | “alternative chains that can lead to different outcomes” |
| [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case) | counterfactual CoT | informal | “the answer is replaced by a stronger-LLM-generated counterfactual answer $y_{i}^{\prime}$ satisfying $y_{i}^{\prime}\neq y_{i}$” |
| [Akter et al. 2025](./#p=akter2025causal) | counterfactual trace | informal | “penalizes the model if predictions remain unchanged” |
| [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto) | counterfactual regularization (perturbed rationales) | informal | “the reasoning module is regularized to output less confident predictions when the rationale is not utilized properly” |
| [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto) | token masking (rationale ignored) | operational | “To simulate this, we mask out the rationales in the input.” |
| [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto) | token replacement (rationale misused) | operational | “we randomly replace $k\%$ of the rationale tokens with other tokens” |
| [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) | counterfactual inputs (bias augmentation) | informal | “a more diverse range of counterfactual inputs than just the presence vs. absence of biasing augmentations” |
| [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) | counterfactual distillation (CD) | formal | “teacher-forces the model’s own neutral response under the trigger” |
| [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) | pressure-aware counterfactual targets | informal | “the model’s own honest response, with a trace that registers the pressure and declines to be moved by it” |
| [Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) | counterfactual-augmented reward model (strategies D and C) | operational | “we append the disclaimer whenever $\operatorname{pred}(y)\neq\operatorname{pred}(y^{\prime})$” |
| [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) | counterfactual consistency (reversed-question pairs) | informal | “counterfactual consistency signals, analogous to those provided by our reversed-question methodology, can substantially improve faithfulness at training time” |

and 2 more: [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) (pressure-aware counterfactual target (ℓ_aware)); [Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto) (counterfactual regularization loss).

Formal versions:
- Counterfactual loss ([Wang et al. 2023](./#p=wang2023scott)): $\mathcal{L}_{counterfactual}=-\sum_i\log P(t_i\mid q,r',t_{<i})$ over the answer tokens of a wrong answer $a'$ that the rationale $r'$ supports.
- Counterfactual regularization ([Wang et al. 2022](./#p=wang2023pinto)): $\mathcal{L}_{\text{c-reg}}=-\sum_{a_i\in A}Q'(a_i\mid q,A)\log P(a_i\mid q,A,R')$, with label-smoothed targets $Q'$ on perturbed rationales $R'$.
- Counterfactual distillation ([Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning)): $\ell_{\mathrm{CD}}(\theta)=-\mathbb{E}_i\log\pi_\theta(y^H_i,t^H_i\mid x_i,c^+_i)$ with $(y^H_i,t^H_i)\sim\pi_0(\cdot\mid x_i,c^-_i)$; the pressure-aware target $\ell_{\mathrm{aware}}$ prepends an acknowledgment $a(c^+_i)$ to $t^H_i$.

### 11. Counterfactual internal states and parameters

The counterfactual is an alternative value of something inside the model:
- an attention distribution, permuted or adversarially searched with the encoder states held fixed ([Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention)), and the same framework as read by [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention);
- activations taken from a counterfactual input and patched into the original run ([Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training), [Bhupatiraju & Nyaupane 2026](./#p=bhupatiraju2026stated), [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective));
- an edited knowledge triple, injected in context or by weight editing ([Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal));
- a token-permuted span at which a Jacobian is taken ([Wang et al. 2025a](./#p=wang2025causallyenhanced));
- the model's parameters after a reasoning step is unlearned ([Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring)).

In the patching rows, the counterfactual input serves only as the source of the activations. Two rows change the weights: [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) unlearn a reasoning step to obtain a modified model $\mathcal{M}^{*}$, and [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) inject edited knowledge with MEMIT, which is "directly modifying specific model weights", as well as in context. 11 rows from 8 papers (8 proposed, 3 adopted).

> scenarios corresponding to alternative (counterfactual) attention weights. The idea is to investigate whether the prediction would have been different, had the model emphasized (attended to) different input features.
>
> ([Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention), §4.2)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) | counterfactual attention weights | informal | “whether the prediction would have been different, had the model emphasized (attended to) different input features” |
| [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) | adversarial attention | operational | “maximally distinct from $\hat{\alpha}$ but that nonetheless yields an equivalent prediction” |
| [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) | attention permutation (counterfactual attention) | operational | “we simply scramble the original attention weights $\hat{\alpha}$” |
| [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention) | counterfactual attention weights (counterfactual framework) | criterion | “if a model is capable of producing multiple sets of diverse attention weights for the same prediction, then the relationship between inputs and outputs used to make predictions is not understood by attention analysis” |
| [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) | counterfactual input (activation patching) | informal | “a counterfactual sentence $x^{\prime}$ with the same relation but differing subject and object” |
| [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) | activation patching (intervention T_patch) | formal | “replace the activation $h_{\ell,t}(x)$ with the corresponding activation $h_{\ell,t}(x^{\prime})$” |
| [Bhupatiraju & Nyaupane 2026](./#p=bhupatiraju2026stated) | counterfactual (per-hop knowledge-base edit) | informal | “a single fact resolving hop $k$ which is further altered” |
| [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective) | counterfactual inputs (source/target pair for activation patching) | operational | “share the same hint cue $C=C^{\prime}$ , but have different cue-included behavioral responses” |
| [Zaman & Srivastava 2025a](./#p=zaman2025causal) | counterfactual (knowledge edit) | informal | “These counterfactuals yield the same answer but are based on different reasoning.” |
| [Wang et al. 2025a](./#p=wang2025causallyenhanced) | source-side counterfactual (token permutation) | operational | “permuting the tokens of $A$ (and of $M$ when present)” |
| [Tutek et al. 2025](./#p=tutek2025measuring) | parameter intervention (unlearning a reasoning step) | informal | “its modified version $\mathcal{M}^{*}$ should not produce the same predictions or reasoning that $\mathcal{M}$ did” |

Formal versions:
- Activation patching ([Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training)): $\mathcal{M}(x;\,h_{\ell,t}(x)\leftarrow h_{\ell,t}(x'))$, scored by $d(\mathcal{M}(x),\mathcal{M}(x;\,h_{\ell,t}(x)\leftarrow h_{\ell,t}(x')))$.
- Adversarial attention ([Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention)): the largest JSD from $\hat\alpha$ among weights whose output stays within $\epsilon$ TVD of the original, with $\epsilon=0.01$ for classification and $0.05$ for QA.

### 12. Counterfactual baselines for scheming, obfuscation and dishonesty

In these rows the counterfactual condition is the evidence that a behavior is strategic, hidden or dishonest, in settings where a single trajectory does not show it. The rows cover six behaviors:
- sandbagging: the environment's incentive is reversed, and the behavior is compared across the two conditions ([Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress));
- sabotage: the same coding task is framed as for the model itself or for its successor, and "o4-mini SAB underperforms on its successor’s code at a significantly higher rate than on its own" ([Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress));
- evaluation awareness: awareness is injected into or removed from the CoT to measure its "causal effect ... on the pursuit of covert actions" ([Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress));
- steganographic reliance: an ordinary RL agent that "learns to evade the monitor to secretly encode information" shows a large gap between the original and the "counterfactual" reward when only the applicant's nationality is changed ([Farquhar et al. 2025](./#p=farquhar2025mona));
- obfuscated denial: a judge asks whether reasoning "could ... plausibly have been produced in a scenario where no defect existed"; it labels "93.8% of behavior-only CoTs as impossible without the defect, compared to only 38.4% for the monitor-aware model" ([Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training));
- dishonesty: a neutral variant of each query verifies what the model knows before honesty is scored under pressure ([Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating)); biased and unbiased prompts verify "whether specific factors actually influenced behavior" for an honest-persona reviewer ([Dietz et al. 2026](./#p=dietz2026split)); a persona system prompt tests whether denial of tampering reflects "a fixed “safe and reliable assistant” persona" ([Nguyen et al. 2026](./#p=nguyen2026can)).

These rows are where the counterfactual family meets the scheming and obfuscation rows of the [monitorability](#monitorability) section and the honesty rows of the [faithfulness](#faithfulness) section. 8 rows from 6 papers (8 proposed).

> The non-counterfactual condition incentivizes low performance by stating that high performance leads to negative outcomes, while the counterfactual condition reverses this incentive.
>
> ([Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress), App. F.3)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) | counterfactual condition | operational | “The non-counterfactual condition incentivizes low performance by stating that high performance leads to negative outcomes, while the counterfactual condition reverses this incentive.” |
| [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) | self vs. successor counterfactual | operational | “we compare the self vs. successor counterfactual” |
| [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) | counterfactual interventions on the CoT | informal | “counterfactual interventions on the CoT, where we inject or remove situational awareness” |
| [Farquhar et al. 2025](./#p=farquhar2025mona) | counterfactual reward (nationality intervention) | operational | “applications that are identical to the training data apart from nationality” |
| [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) | counterfactual autorater | operational | “could the model’s reasoning plausibly have been produced in a scenario where no defect existed?” |
| [Wang et al. 2025b](./#p=wang2025evaluating) | counterfactual samples (neutral query variant) | informal | “the need to verify the model’s knowledge using counterfactual samples” |
| [Dietz et al. 2026](./#p=dietz2026split) | A/B prompt method (biased vs unbiased system prompt) | operational | “by comparing responses to biased versus unbiased prompts, we can verify whether specific factors actually influenced behavior” |
| [Nguyen et al. 2026](./#p=nguyen2026can) | persona counterfactual | operational | “a fixed “safe and reliable assistant” persona that claims its outputs and denies tampering whatever the prior turn contained” |

### Lineage

Of the 292 rows, 132 are borrowed (86 adopted, 46 adapted), from 62 papers. Of these, 76 cite at least one source in the map and 56 cite only sources outside it. Counting each row once per cited source, 13 sources are borrowed by at least 5 rows:

| Source | Rows | Papers | In the map | Cluster with most rows |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Atanasova et al. 2023 | 18 | 14 | no | 8 (13 rows) |
| [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) | 17 | 10 | yes | 2 (14 rows) |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | 15 | 9 | yes | 9 (13 rows) |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | 12 | 9 | yes | 5 (9 rows) |
| Siegel et al. 2024 | 9 | 8 | no | 8 (8 rows) |
| Pearl (2001, 2009, 2021, 2022) | 9 | 7 | no | 6 (3 rows) |
| [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) | 7 | 4 | yes | 5 (3 rows) |
| Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 | 7 | 6 | no | 2 (7 rows) |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | 5 | 4 | yes | 5 (3 rows) |
| [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) | 5 | 5 | yes | 2 (2 rows) |
| [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) | 5 | 4 | yes | 1 (3 rows) |
| Hase & Bansal 2020 | 5 | 3 | no | 2 (5 rows) |
| [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) | 5 | 5 | yes | 9 (4 rows) |

Eight of the 13 sources are in the map and five are not. The most-borrowed source, Atanasova et al. 2023, is outside the map, and so is Siegel et al. 2024, the second source of the same cluster. Atanasova et al. and [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) are borrowed by 18 and 17 rows, but 13 of the Atanasova rows fall in cluster 8 and 14 of the Chen rows in cluster 2, and no row cites both. [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) come closest: they list Atanasova et al. and Siegel et al. among works that "measure faithfulness through the framework of counterfactual simulatability".

The chains of borrowing:
- **Simulatability.** Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 and Hase & Bansal 2020 are cited by [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) for the concept's "long history". Chen et al. are borrowed by 10 papers: [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language), [Agarwal et al. 2024](./#p=agarwal2024faithfulness), [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent), [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek), [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain), [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching), [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk), [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm), [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) and [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would). Two second-hand links follow: [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) to [Anonymous 2026 (introspective oversight)](./#p=anon2026trainingoversight), and [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) to [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient). [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) cite Doshi-Velez & Kim directly and are borrowed by 5 papers: [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain), [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective), [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would), [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal) and [Pres et al. 2026b](./#p=pres2026selfctrl).
- **Self-generated counterfactual explanations.** Wachter et al. 2017 supply the definition in [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) and [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms). The self-generated form runs through four papers: [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) to [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) (adapted), to [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms), to [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating). Madsen et al. are also borrowed directly by [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating), [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) and [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating).
- **Counterfactual test.** Atanasova et al. 2023 are borrowed by 14 papers, Siegel et al. 2024 by 8 and Siegel et al. 2025 by 2 ([Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did), [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training)). [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) merge the test with the hint pairs of [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) and cite both.
- **Cue pairs.** [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) are borrowed by 9 papers, [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) by 4 ([Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness), [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length), [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm)) and [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) by 2 ([Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual)). [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) are borrowed by [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought), [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight) and [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability), and Xiong et al. in turn by [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight). [Li et al. 2025b](./#p=li2025training) are borrowed by [Guo et al. 2026](./#p=guo2026introspective), and [Gema et al. 2026](./#p=gema2026chainofthought) by [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient).
- **Concept edits.** [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) are borrowed by [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did), [Luo et al. 2026](./#p=luo2026removal), [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) and [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating).
- **Reasoning interventions.** [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) are borrowed by 9 papers: [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning), [Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp), [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace), [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring), [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training), [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift), [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self), [Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop) and [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting). [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) are borrowed by [Kudo et al. 2024](./#p=kudo2024llms), [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting), [Wang et al. 2026a](./#p=wang2026case), [Wang et al. 2026b](./#p=wang2026concept) and [Zaman & Srivastava 2025b](./#p=zaman2025chainofthought); [Han et al. 2026](./#p=han2026rfeval) by [Lv et al. 2026](./#p=lv2026geofaith); Bogdan et al. 2025 by [Macar et al. 2025](./#p=macar2025thought).
- **Others.** [Jain & Wallace 2019](./#p=jain2019attention) are borrowed by [Wiegreffe & Pinter 2019](./#p=wiegreffe2019attention), and [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) by [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) and [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning).

Six kinds of drift appear as the definitions are passed on.

1. **Simulatability changes status.** [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) ask whether an explanation lets humans "precisely infer the model’s outputs on diverse counterfactuals of the explained input", and write that "simulatability is a special case of faithfulness" and "a faithful explanation is not necessarily simulatable". [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) call it "The counterfactual simulatability framework of explanation faithfulness". In [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) it becomes a biconditional: "an explanation is faithful if and only if the explanation allows a human or model to accurately predict how the model will behave on other relevant inputs". [Hong & Roth 2026](./#p=hong2026llm) change what is predicted, to "how a model’s prediction would change on counterfactual inputs", and call the property usefulness. [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) change the counterfactuals: Chen et al. are said to argue that "a good explanation should help an observer predict behavior under counterfactual edits". Three things shift along the chain: the observer goes from humans to "a human or model", the counterfactuals go from LLM-written related questions to edits, and the notion goes from a special case of faithfulness to a necessary and sufficient condition for it. The attribution check records the biconditional as an adaptation of Chen et al.
2. **The counterfactual explanation acquires a target, changes author and loses the target again.** In [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can), after Wachter et al., it is an input that "would lead the model to change its decision". [Mayne et al. 2025](./#p=mayne2025llms) tie it to "a specific alternative output", and [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) to "the opposite prediction". The edit is then no longer found by an optimizer: [Dehghanighobadi et al. 2025](./#p=dehghanighobadi2025can) "ask the model itself to generate the counterfactual explanation", and [Doi et al. 2025](./#p=doi2025investigating) add "editing-distance constraints specified in a prompt". [Zeng et al. 2026](./#p=zeng2026evaluating) recast it as "the propose-and-verify form of Edit-Proposal" and drop the target label: the edit counts as a flip if "the fraction of resamples that differ from baseline exceeds $0.5$".
3. **The counterfactual test widens.** [Parcalabescu & Frank 2023](./#p=parcalabescu2023measuring) describe Atanasova et al. as training "a helper model to insert words into the LLM input". [Wang & Atanasova 2025](./#p=wang2025selfcritique) generate the inserted modifiers with GPT-4o. [Gema et al. 2026](./#p=gema2026chainofthought) describe counterfactual approaches as testing explanations "through input edits, reconstruction, or output-distribution shifts", [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) as using "auxiliary models to generate counterfactual texts designed to change the LLM outcome", and [Cheah et al. 2026](./#p=cheah2026training) as using "controlled interventions, such as the introduction of biasing hints". By this point the label "counterfactual test" also covers the hint pairs that [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) had filed under counterfactual simulatability. [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) restate word insertion as a counterfactual set and list it among "Irrelevant or incoherent perturbations".
4. **Borrowers add the word.** [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) use "counterfactual" once in the text, for the cited work of Madaan & Yazdanbakhsh. Of the 15 rows that borrow from Lanham et al., 6 rows from 5 papers ([Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning), [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace), [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift), [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self), [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting)) call its tests counterfactual in the quoted passage, and [Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop) name truncation "prefix counterfactuals". [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) use the word once, for Paul et al., yet [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) write of the same hint-flip filter "we regard this as a counterfactual". [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring) never use the word, and [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) list them among works that "apply counterfactual interventions to input features".
5. **The cue-removal label tightens in one line and loosens in another.** [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) "primarily use greedy decoding" for the original and counterfactual inputs. [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) take their cue types but label with 10 samples per condition, counting a case positive only if "the cue raises the rate of the cue-consistent answer by at least 50pp". In the other direction, [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) hold that "we cannot label individual samples as having been causally influenced" unless the base rate is zero, while [Ferreira et al. 2026](./#p=ferreira2026outofsight), who cite Guan et al., use "the counterfactual prediction without the hint" to get "a per-example target label".
6. **Lanham et al.'s tests are re-parameterized and extended.** [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) truncate the CoT "after each step (here, each sentence)"; [Anonymous 2026 (GRACE)](./#p=anon2026grace) truncate "at 25/50/75% token levels". [Anonymous 2026 (CRISP)](./#p=anon2026crisp) have a generator write a chain with "exactly ONE decisive error". [Parekh & Goyal 2026](./#p=parekh2026drop) decode greedily to find a commitment point. [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) list five tests under Lanham et al., two of which (Option Shuffling and Removing Steps) Lanham et al. do not run (see the attribution check).

### Attribution check

Of the 132 borrowed rows, 76 cite at least one source in the map. They give 96 entry-source pairs from 43 papers against 22 sources, and each pair was checked against the source's full text. The check covers every such pair in this family, so the counts below are rates for the family, not for a sample.

| Verdict | Pairs | Share |
|---|---|---|
| supported | 49 | 51% |
| adapted | 32 | 33% |
| partial | 7 | 7% |
| renamed | 5 | 5% |
| unsupported | 2 | 2% |
| misdirected | 1 | 1% |
| other-claim | 0 | 0% |
| **total** | **96** | |

The verdicts support six conclusions.

1. **84% of the checked borrowings hold.** 81 of the 96 pairs are supported or adapted. The 15 others fall on 14 rows from 11 papers; [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) account for 4 of them and [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) for 2. The stance field and the verdict disagree on 7 pairs: 4 pairs on rows marked adopted came back adapted, and 3 pairs on rows marked adapted came back supported.
2. **[Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) is the source with the most failed checks.** Of its 15 pairs, 6 are not supported (2 unsupported, 3 partial, 1 renamed), against 1 of 17 for [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) and 1 of 12 for [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language); all 5 pairs on [Paul et al. 2024](./#p=paul2024making) are supported. [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) cite Lanham et al. alone for Option Shuffling and Removing Steps, which Lanham et al. do not run, and for a Paraphrasing test that rewrites steps "while preserving or slightly altering their semantics", where Lanham et al. instruct "conveying exactly the same information". [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) list Lanham et al. among works that "apply counterfactual interventions to input features", but Lanham et al. write "We intervene on the CoT produced by the model rather than few shot prompt examples". [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) describe the Adding Mistakes test as a check on "mathematical counterfactuals", although Lanham et al. run it on all eight of their tasks. [Li et al. 2025a](./#p=li2025drift) cite Lanham et al. with Atanasova et al. for modifying "key parts w of the inputs I"; the check records this pair as adapted, since the input edit comes from Atanasova et al.
3. **[Hase et al. 2020](./#p=hase2020leakageadjusted) is cited for a notion it does not define.** Both papers that cite it for counterfactual simulatability, [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) (misdirected) and [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm) (partial), fail the check. Its simulator predicts the output on the explained input, with correctness $\mathbf{1}[\hat{y}_i\mid x_i,\hat{e}_i]$, and the word "counterfactual" does not occur in its text. [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) place it among works in which "the simulation input is the explained input" and cite Hase & Bansal 2020, which is outside the map, for the concept's "long history". The second use of Hase et al. 2020 by [Mayne et al. 2026](./#p=mayne2026positivecasefaithfulnessllm), for a baseline "without explanation access", does match the source.
4. **The five renamings run in two directions.** Three give the name "counterfactual" to an operation whose source does not use the word for it: [Alon et al. 2026](./#p=alon2026faithfulness) for the hint filter of [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning), [Madsen et al. 2024](./#p=madsen2024self) for the Adding Mistakes test of [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring), and [Admoni et al. 2025](./#p=admoni2026aligning) for the input perturbations of [Wiegreffe et al. 2021](./#p=wiegreffe2021measuring). Two rename a measure: [Chen et al. 2024](./#p=chen2025consistent) call the simulation precision of [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl) "explanation consistency", and [Tang & Khanna 2026](./#p=tang2026unlearning) call the bias-augmented consistency training of [Chua et al. 2024](./#p=chua2024biasaugmented) "counterfactual distillation".
5. **Three partial verdicts concern a detail of the source.** [Karvonen et al. 2026](./#p=karvonen2026would) place the suggested-answer cue of [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) on "an MMLU question", but Turpin et al. use BIG-Bench Hard and BBQ. [Arcuschin et al. 2025](./#p=arcuschin2025chain) credit [Hase & Potts 2026](./#p=hase2026counterfactual) with "$+35$ pp monitoring accuracy using question-inversion counterfactuals", but Hase & Potts report the 35-point gain in monitor accuracy for cue-based counterfactuals, and a 2-point gain in simulatability for their model-generated counterfactuals, which include inverted questions. [Bhan et al. 2025](./#p=bhan2025did) group [Matton et al. 2025](./#p=matton2025walk) with methods that judge "a self-NLE to justify its new prediction", while Matton et al. "consider only the explanations for original questions".
6. **The biconditional of [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) is an adaptation, checked against one of three sources.** The check records it as adapted from [Chen et al. 2023](./#p=pmlr-v235-chen24bl), who write that "a faithful explanation is not necessarily simulatable". The two other sources Turpin et al. cite for it, Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 and Hase & Bansal 2020, are outside the map and were not checked.

One misattribution falls outside the checks because the extracted row quotes a different passage. In §1, [Shen et al. 2026](./#p=shen2026detecting) cite [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) (their [40], with three other works) for "counterfactual interventions , where parts of the CoT are perturbed to examine their effect on the final prediction"; Turpin et al. perturb only the input, comparing predictions on "unmodified inputs" with predictions on "inputs that have a biasing feature applied". The sources outside the map have no cached text and were not checked: Atanasova et al. 2023 (18 rows), Siegel et al. 2024 (9) and 2025 (4), Pearl (9), Doshi-Velez & Kim 2017 (7), Hase & Bansal 2020 (5) and Wachter et al. 2017 (4).
