### Monitorability and legibility taxes

9 rows from 9 papers (8 informal, 1 operational; 4 proposed, 4 adopted, 1 adapted).

These rows name the cost of keeping a model monitorable or legible, and the payer and the unit change from paper to paper. [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) measure the capability lost by switching to a smaller model at the same compute; [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) describe costs that "frontier model developers may be required to pay"; [Kirchner et al. 2024](./#p=kirchner2024proververifier) report the test performance lost to legibility training; [Google DeepMind 2026](./#p=google2026gemini4argon) names a "capability tax" that evasion imposes on the model; [Li 2026](./#p=li2026monitorability) calls the oversight cost of compressing the CoT "a mechanism-dependent tax"; and [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing) measure an alignment tax of the MI reward.

> frontier model developers may be required to pay some cost, i.e. a monitorability tax [33], such as deploying slightly less performant models or suffering more expensive inference, in order to maintain the monitorability of our agents.
>
> ([Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring), §1)

| Paper | Term | Kind | Definition |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | monitorability tax | informal | "such as deploying slightly less performant models or suffering more expensive inference" |
| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | monitorability tax | informal | "The capability cost incurred from switching to a smaller model using the same amount of compute" |
| [Kirchner et al. 2024](./#p=kirchner2024proververifier) | legibility tax | informal | "lower test performance than the prover optimized for correctness alone, which represents an instance of a legibility tax" |
| [Kim & Lee 2026](./#p=kim2026mitigating) | legibility tax | informal | "a degradation in accuracy compared to a baseline trained only to maximize correctness" |
| [Google DeepMind 2026](./#p=google2026gemini4argon) | capability tax | informal | "evasion imposes a capability tax: when the model modifies its thoughts to avoid detection, the quality of its technical exploits declines" |
| [Li 2026](./#p=li2026monitorability) | monitorability tax (mechanism-dependent) | informal | "not a fixed length– monitorability law but a mechanism-dependent tax" |
| [Cui et al. 2026](./#p=cui2026behavior) | monitorability tax | informal | "reasoning is deliberately left outside the training loop to explicitly isolate it from optimization pressures" |
| [Little 2026](./#p=little2026length) | monitorability tax | informal | "a “monitorability tax” that keeps the trace readable" |
| [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing) | alignment tax (of the MI reward) | operational | *loss of true pass rate and monitor accuracy from optimizing $R_{\mathrm{MI}}$ with no monitor pressure* |

### Lineage

382 of the 1,169 rows (33%), from 88 papers, name at least one source for their definition. They cite 179 distinct sources: 33 are papers in the map, cited 281 times, and 146 are outside it, cited 254 times. 288 rows cite one source and 94 cite two or more.

The twelve most-cited sources:

| Source | Citing rows | Citing papers | Clusters of the citing rows |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | 74 | 18 | evaluation designs 31, metrics 24, predictive 9, rubrics 2 |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | 51 | 39 | obfuscation 17, verbalization 9, monitors 9, predictive 7 |
| [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) | 29 | 27 | monitors 8, verbalization 6, predictive 6, obfuscation 4 |
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) | 28 | 17 | necessity 12, predictive 4, monitors 3, encoded reasoning 3 |
| Kutasov et al. 2025 (not in map) | 15 | 9 | scheming 6, evaluation designs 2, monitors 2, rubrics 2 |
| Greenblatt et al. 2024 (not in map) | 15 | 12 | scheming 8, monitors 3, evaluation designs 1, self-reports 1 |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | 12 | 7 | evaluation designs 7, verbalization 3, obfuscation 1, reward hacking 1 |
| [Roger & Greenblatt 2023](./#p=roger2023preventing) | 12 | 7 | encoded reasoning 11, obfuscation 1 |
| [Kirchner et al. 2024](./#p=kirchner2024proververifier) | 11 | 7 | legibility 9, verbalization 1, taxes 1 |
| Arnav et al. 2025 (not in map) | 10 | 6 | scheming 3, monitors 2, evasion 2, evaluation designs 1 |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | 8 | 5 | encoded reasoning 5, verbalization 2, necessity 1 |
| Chen et al. 2026 (not in map) | 7 | 4 | evasion 6, monitors 1 |

Four papers in the map supply 182 of the 535 row-source citations, and they are borrowed for different things. [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) is cited for its apparatus: 31 of its 74 citing rows are in the evaluation-design cluster and 24 in the metrics cluster. [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) is cited for concepts and reaches the most papers (39): 17 of its 51 citing rows are in the obfuscation cluster. [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) (29 rows, 27 papers) is cited across monitors, verbalization and predictive rows, and [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) (28 rows, 17 papers) for necessity (12 rows).

Baker et al.'s definition travels in two directions. 7 rows in the predictive cluster cite it for prediction of a property ([Kaufmann et al. 2026](./#p=kaufmann2026aligned): "independent of the model’s “true reasoning process”"), and 9 rows in the verbalization cluster cite it for a faithfulness requirement ([Occhipinti et al. 2026](./#p=occhipinti2026probing): "Faithfulness is a prerequisite for CoT monitorability"; [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability): "faithfully reflect the model’s internal decision-making path"). The attribution check marks 13 of these 16 restatements supported, 1 adapted and 2 partial, because Baker's text holds both readings: monitorability is "the ability to monitor for specific properties of agents actions or outputs (monitorability), and not faithfulness in isolation", and "If a monitor $M$ can accurately predict property $v$ from latent $z$, then $z$ must be faithful with respect to $v$ and legible to $M$". The two partial verdicts are the restatements that extend faithfulness from the monitored property to the whole reasoning process: [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) and [Reddy et al. 2025](./#p=reddy2025principled) ("the ability to inspect and evaluate a model’s internal reasoning process").

Guan et al.'s definition changes in smaller steps. [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) quote it verbatim; [Anonymous 2026 (improving monitorability)](./#p=anon2026improving) widen the reader to "the monitoring setup"; [Lewis-Lim et al. 2026](./#p=lewislim2026efficient) and [OpenAI 2026](./#p=openai2026gpt6astra) replace "predict" with "recovered" and "infer"; and [Li 2026](./#p=li2026monitorability) adds that "monitorability is fragile under scaling and training pressure", which the check marks partial because Guan et al. report that "RL optimization does not materially decrease monitorability even at the current frontier scale". Its archetypes are renamed in five checked rows: [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) call them "monitorability types" and the intervention archetype "influence", [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026monitorbench) turn intervention and process into the axes "input intervention" and "solution process", and [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability)'s "Grader Hacking Hint" is the category Guan et al. call reward hacking.

No row extracted from [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) defines monitorability itself: its 21 rows define the CoT monitor, the necessity and the propensity to think out loud, the externalized reasoning property, evaluations of and threats to monitorability, and state that "CoT does not need to completely represent the actual reasoning process". Of the 12 rows in the two definitional clusters that cite Korbak et al., the check marks 9 supported and 3 adapted, including the faithfulness wordings of [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability), [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) and [Anonymous 2026 (one draw)](./#p=anon2026one), which match Korbak's threat that optimization "might make this chain of thought less faithfully reflect how models arrive at their decisions".

Emmons et al.'s necessity reaches the citing rows under its own terms ("CoT uplift", the with-CoT minus no-CoT pass rate, and "CoT-as-computation", which [Jose et al. 2026](./#p=jose2026strategic) and [Golechha et al. 2026](./#p=golechha2026preliminary) adopt) and under two new names: "CoT reliance" in [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought) (renamed, per the check) and "load-bearing" reasoning in [Duzan & Cooper Stickland 2026](./#p=duzan2026chainofthought). It is also cited for an instrument it does not contain: [MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning) attribute the legibility and coverage autorater to it, and the check finds the autorater in [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic).

Outside the map, Kutasov et al. 2025 (15 rows, 9 papers) and Greenblatt et al. 2024 (15 rows, 12 papers) are the most cited, with 6 and 8 of their citing rows in the scheming cluster. In the four behavior clusters (deception, scheming, reward hacking, evaluation awareness), 87 rows name a source and 10 of them cite a paper in the map, so 77 borrowed behavior definitions fall outside the attribution check.

### Attribution check

Every row whose source list includes a paper in the map was checked against that paper: 281 entry-source pairs on 216 rows from 70 papers. The other 166 rows that name a source cite only sources outside the map and are not checked.

| Verdict | Checks | Meaning |
|---|---:|---|
| supported | 169 | the source gives the definition as the citing paper states it |
| adapted | 68 | the source gives it and the citing paper changes it |
| partial | 21 | the source supports part of what is attributed to it |
| renamed | 15 | the source gives the notion under another name |
| unsupported | 3 | the source does not give it |
| misdirected | 3 | another paper gives it, not the one cited |
| other-claim | 2 | the citation supports a different claim than the definition |
| total | 281 | |

Sources checked at least five times:

| Source | Checks | Supported | Adapted | Not supported |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | 74 | 48 | 18 | 8 |
| [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | 51 | 34 | 8 | 9 |
| [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) | 29 | 25 | 3 | 1 |
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) | 28 | 17 | 7 | 4 |
| [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) | 12 | 6 | 4 | 2 |
| [Roger & Greenblatt 2023](./#p=roger2023preventing) | 12 | 6 | 4 | 2 |
| [Kirchner et al. 2024](./#p=kirchner2024proververifier) | 11 | 3 | 7 | 1 |
| [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | 8 | 5 | 1 | 2 |
| [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) | 6 | 1 | 2 | 3 |
| [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) | 5 | 2 | 3 | 0 |
| [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) | 5 | 3 | 2 | 0 |
| [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) | 5 | 2 | 0 | 3 |

237 of the 281 checks (84%) find the definition in the cited source, as stated (169) or adapted (68); the other 44 fall on 39 rows from 28 papers.

Six checks find that the cited source does not give the definition at all. Three are unsupported: [Chidambaram et al. 2026](./#p=chidambaram2026corrupted) cite [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) for a hint setting that comes from the co-cited [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning); [Jose 2025](./#p=jose2025reasoning) cites [Mathew et al. 2024](./#p=mathew2025hidden) for compression of reasoning under outcome-based RL, which Mathew et al. do not discuss; and [Zhou & Tao 2026](./#p=zhou2026nearzero) cite [Taufeeque et al. 2026](./#p=taufeeque2026obfuscation) for a graded-suspicion text judge that paper does not describe. Three are misdirected: [MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning) cite [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) for the autorater of [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic), and [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) cite [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) for two hints that come from [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning). [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) is the paper that does give the definition in three of the six cases.

Among the 12 sources checked at least five times, the highest shares of failed checks belong to [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) (3 of 5, all renamed) and [Chua & Evans 2025](./#p=chua2025deepseek) (3 of 6: 2 misdirected, 1 renamed); [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) has 1 in 29, and [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) and [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) have none in 5 each.

For [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring), 9 of 51 checks fail, and the 5 partial ones concern what Baker et al. found more than what they defined: [Drori et al. 2025](./#p=drori2025output) write that Baker et al. trained frontier models against a CoT monitor, while the agent trained against it was a non-frontier model; [Singhal & Kumar 2026](./#p=singhal2026believe) write that the pressure made models obfuscate "rather than" change their behavior, while the pressured agent also hacked less; [Zhou & Tao 2026](./#p=zhou2026nearzero) describe a suspiciousness score subtracted from the reward, while Baker et al.'s reward requires passing the tests and not triggering the monitor; and [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) and [Reddy et al. 2025](./#p=reddy2025principled) extend faithfulness to the whole reasoning process.

The 15 renamed verdicts mark a citing paper that gives a source's notion a new name: [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) call [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring)'s perturbation test "causal relevance"; [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought) call [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain)'s CoT uplift "CoT reliance"; [Cui et al. 2026](./#p=cui2026behavior) and [Ferreira et al. 2025](./#p=ferreira2026truthful) rename [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring)'s obfuscated reward hacking; and [MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning), [Kaufmann et al. 2026](./#p=kaufmann2026aligned) and [Lee et al. 2026](./#p=lee2026training) rename notions of [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) ("linguistic drift" in MacDermott et al. and a semantic drift in Kaufmann et al. for what Schoen et al. call unusual terminology, and covert misbehavior in Lee et al. for their scheming and covert actions).

In 11 checks on rows that the extraction marked adopted, the check finds the definition adapted: the citing paper presents a definition as borrowed while changing it. One of them is [Schulz 2026](./#p=schulz2026monitor)'s encoded reasoning, which extends Lanham et al.'s human reader to "a human or LLM monitor", the change the independent audit had flagged.

These verdicts replace the 15 mismatch records of the previous version of this section, and the independent audit's corrections hold. The previous version called 7 records confirmed mismatches; 3 of them, the claims that [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) state the opposite of the faithfulness requirement in [Anonymous 2026 (one draw)](./#p=anon2026one), [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) and [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability), are now supported, since Korbak et al. reject only a complete representation of the reasoning and list optimization that makes the CoT less faithful as a threat. 2 are now partial ([Young 2026](./#p=young2026why) on [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring), [Yu et al. 2026](./#p=yu2026outcome) on [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring)) and 2 stand ([MacDermott et al. 2025](./#p=macdermott2025reasoning), misdirected; [Chidambaram et al. 2026](./#p=chidambaram2026corrupted), unsupported). The two records that called [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) only partial support for [Kaufmann et al. 2026](./#p=kaufmann2026aligned) and [Occhipinti et al. 2026](./#p=occhipinti2026probing) are now supported; the audit judged the Kaufmann record wrong because requiring information about the monitored property does not reinstate a requirement to reproduce the reasoning process. Of the four records left unsettled, [Jose 2025](./#p=jose2025reasoning) on [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) is other-claim (the citation points to the CoT excerpts Baker et al. reproduce, which show the dropped-word style, not to a definition), [Lee et al. 2026](./#p=lee2026training) on [Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) is renamed, [Mao et al. 2026](./#p=mao2026taming) is adapted for [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) and [Drori et al. 2025](./#p=drori2025output) and partial for [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training), and [Skaf et al. 2025](./#p=skaf2025large)'s source is outside the map. The last two records also change: [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain)'s use of faithfulness evaluations as a proxy for monitorability, previously partially supported, is now supported for [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring), [Turpin et al. 2023](./#p=turpin2023language) and [Chen et al. 2025](./#p=chen2025reasoning) and renamed for [Roger 2025](./#p=anthropic2025paraphrases), and [Zhou & Tao 2026](./#p=zhou2026nearzero)'s default monitorability, previously a misattributed term, is adapted from [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring).

| [Chidambaram et al. 2026](./#p=chidambaram2026corrupted) `chidambaram2026corrupted#3` (hint setting (CoT monitorability)) | [Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) | unsupported | Reference [15] is Lanham et al., which uses no hints and no monitor: it intervenes only on the model's own CoT and mentions biasing context only when describing Turpin et al.; the appended-hint, acknowledgment-checking setting comes from the co-cited Chen et al. 2025 [4] (and Chua and Evans [6]). | "Our tests intervene on the model’s stated reasoning in different ways and evaluate how the model’s answer changes in response. ... In that paper, the model produces CoT in the presence of biasing few-shot examples; while the model’s final answer is consistent with the bias provided by the prompt, the CoT gives a different explanation for the answer that does not reference the biasing context." | "we apply investigator agents in the hint setting widely used in prior work on CoT monitorability [15, 4, 6, 3]. | [Arike et al. 2026](./#p=arike2026information) `arike2026information#16` (robust CoT-monitoring safety case) | [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) | partial | Emmons et al. support requirement (2), since their sketched safety case rests on CoT being necessary for severe harm, but requirement (1), "track the monitorability of their models and treat it as a contributor to safety", is the Section 4 heading of Korbak et al. (2025) (korbak2025chain); Emmons et al. only recommend continued stress testing of monitorability. | "instead of arguing that models are incapable of top-human level stealth, a safety case could argue that models cannot perform top-human level stealth without being caught by a CoT monitor. ... We recommend continued stress testing of CoT monitorability, caution against training practices that might reduce the transparency of model reasoning, and emphasize the safety benefits of architectures with externalized CoT over alternatives with latent CoT." | "To verify that CoT monitors can provide robust safety guarantees, AI developers should thus (1) track the monitorability of their models and treat it as a contributor to safety, and (2) provide evidence that a given model is incapable of executing catastrophic actions without CoT reasoning (Emmons et al., 2025)." |

| [Li 2026](./#p=li2026monitorability) `li2026monitorability#2` (monitorability) | [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | partial | Guan et al. do measure how well a monitor predicts a property from the trace, but they report that frontier-scale RL does not materially reduce monitorability and that it generally rises with inference compute; they find drops only under deliberately rewarded CoT obfuscation, and 'fragile' appears only as a stated concern. | "However, this “monitorability” may be fragile under different training procedures, data sources, or even continued system scaling. [...] These results suggest that reinforcement learning does not meaningfully degrade monitorability, even at the frontier scale at OpenAI; however, we underscore that these results only represent two experiments and more thorough study is needed. [...] To probe this, we modified a standard internal RL setup to explicitly reward CoT obfuscation in various ways" | "A parallel line of work quantifies how far a monitor can recover a latent variable from the reasoning trace, and reports that monitorability is fragile under scaling and training pressure (Guan et al., 2025; MacDermott et al., 2025)." |

These results show that models with both the means (CoT control) and motive (monitor knowledge) can obfuscate their reasoning, even without optimization pressure. ... Monitor-aware models placed under equal reinforcement learning optimization pressure also learn to reward-hack without triggering a CoT monitor substantially faster than unaware controls." | "CoT obfuscation means an operational, non-intentional training outcome in which reward or accuracy rises while operational monitorability declines (Guan et al., 2025; Drori et al., 2025; Haskins et al., 2026)." |

We conclude that CST improves CoT monitorability and simulatability." | "CoT monitoring treats reasoning traces as a safety-relevant channel: weaker models can often supervise stronger ones through their CoT, yet RL and long benign reasoning erode this signal (Baker et al., 2025; Korbak et al., 2025; Emmons et al., 2025; Chen et al., 2026b; Anwar et al., 2025; Zhao et al., 2025; Hase and Potts, 2026; Zolkowski et al., 2025; Wang et al., 2026; Haskins et al., 2026)." |

Monitor-aware models placed under equal reinforcement learning optimization pressure also learn to reward-hack without triggering a CoT monitor substantially faster than unaware controls." | "CoT monitoring treats reasoning traces as a safety-relevant channel: weaker models can often supervise stronger ones through their CoT, yet RL and long benign reasoning erode this signal (Baker et al., 2025; Korbak et al., 2025; Emmons et al., 2025; Chen et al., 2026b; Anwar et al., 2025; Zhao et al., 2025; Hase and Potts, 2026; Zolkowski et al., 2025; Wang et al., 2026; Haskins et al., 2026)." |
| [Reddy et al. 2025](./#p=reddy2025principled) `reddy2025principled#1` (CoT monitorability) | [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | partial | Baker et al. do present CoTs as a window into the agent's decision-making that is useful for safety, but they define monitorability as monitoring specific properties of actions or outputs and explicitly contrast it with evaluating the model's reasoning process (faithfulness), which they call impossible to fully represent and not actionable. | "We argue that ultimately it is the ability to monitor for specific properties of agents actions or outputs (monitorability), and not faithfulness in isolation, that is important. [...] This is quite a stringent requirement as it is likely impossible to represent the entire reasoning process unless the latent considered is the full set of activations. Besides the stringency of the definition, it is also not very actionable: how does one determine what the true reasoning process is? [...] As a result, it is plausible that CoTs will reveal critical insights into the agent’s decision-making in language naturally understandable by both humans and LLMs, and we empirically find this to often be the case." | "monitorability refers to the ability to inspect and evaluate a model’s internal reasoning process through its generated Chain-of-Thought (CoT) traces, providing a window into how the model arrives at its final answer and serving as a natural tool for alignment and safety auditing (Baker et al., 2025; Korbak et al., 2025)." |
| [Roytburg et al. 2026](./#p=roytburg2026measuring) `roytburg2026measuring#3` (legibility (prior definitions)) | [Kirchner et al. 2024](./#p=kirchner2024proververifier) | partial | The source supports the soundness half (a smaller verifier rejecting incorrect solutions from sneaky provers) but not the claim that this defines legibility: the source calls a prover legible only when completeness also holds, i.e. the verifier accepts the helpful prover's correct solutions. | "Checkability — i.e. the capacity of a verifier to independently check the correctness of solutions generated by a helpful prover — can be defined by a combination of two properties: [...] Completeness. For any input in the domain of interest, the helpful prover generates correct solutions and the verifier accepts them as correct. [...] Soundness. The verifier rejects all incorrect solutions produced by any adversarial provers of the same model class as the helpful one. [...] When we find helpful provers and verifiers with such limited completeness and soundness properties, we say that the helpful prover is legible to the verifier." | "Prior work defines legibility inconsistently. Kirchner et al. (2024) define it by how well weak verifiers can invalidate misaligned responses." |
| [Singhal & Kumar 2026](./#p=singhal2026believe) `singhal2026believe#4` (obfuscation) | [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | partial | Baker et al. find obfuscation under CoT pressure, but not instead of behavior change: the pressured agent hacked less overall and produced more genuine solutions, and in the low-optimization regime the pressure gave more aligned agents. | "In Figure 4 we see that by directly putting optimization pressure on the chain-of-thought we do indeed produce a more performant and more aligned agent: it both produces more non-hacking, correct solutions and hacks less overall. [...] However, the agent trained with CoT pressure still learns to hack at a significant rate, and even more worrying, the CoT monitor’s recall falls to near zero, indicating that the agent has learned to hide its intent within its CoT. [...] While we show that integrating CoT monitors into the reinforcement learning reward can indeed produce more capable and more aligned agents in the low optimization regime" | "Baker et al. extended this line of work by showing that penalizing undesirable chain-of-thought during training causes models to obfuscate their reasoning rather than change their behavior (Baker et al., 2025)." |
| [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) `xiong2026monitorability#1` (monitorable (reasoning)) | [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | partial | Baker et al. do require a monitored latent to be faithful and legible to the monitor, but only faithful with respect to the monitored property; they argue that reflecting the whole reasoning process is neither feasible nor what monitorability needs. | "If a monitor $M$ can accurately predict property $v$ from latent $z$ , then $z$ must be faithful with respect to $v$ and legible to $M$ . [...] Intuitively, this is the same definition as “representing the reasoning process behind a model’s prediction” though restricted to only a particular aspect, $v$ , of the reasoning. [...] This is quite a stringent requirement as it is likely impossible to represent the entire reasoning process unless the latent considered is the full set of activations. Besides the stringency of the definition, it is also not very actionable: how does one determine what the true reasoning process is? [...] We argue that ultimately it is the ability to monitor for specific properties of agents actions or outputs (monitorability), and not faithfulness in isolation, that is important." | "For this promise to hold in practice, however, the generated reasoning must be monitorable: the generated reasoning traces should faithfully reflect the model’s internal decision-making path in a form that is legible to external auditors or automated oversight systems (Korbak et al., 2025; Baker et al., 2025; Chen et al., 2025a; Xiong et al., 2025; Arnav et al., 2025; Emmons et al., 2025). A highly monitorable model produces reasoning traces that closely track its latent computation" |
| [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) `xiong2026monitorability#1` (monitorable (reasoning)) | [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) | partial | Emmons et al. support the legibility and oversight parts of the definition (a reader uses the CoT to detect bad actions; monitorability fails if reasoning becomes illegible), but not the requirement that traces faithfully reflect the internal decision path, which they set aside in favor of monitorability. | "Monitorability: We say a CoT is monitorable to a reader (human or automated) if the reader can use the CoT to determine if the model is taking bad actions. ... Monitorability would fail if a model’s reasoning becomes opaque or illegible to an auditor. ... In contrast, our work argues for a shift in focus from faithfulness to monitorability: whether we can use the CoT to detect harmful behavior, even if the CoT is not a perfect representation of the model’s internal reasoning (Baker et al., 2025)." | "the generated reasoning must be monitorable: the generated reasoning traces should faithfully reflect the model’s internal decision-making path in a form that is legible to external auditors or automated oversight systems (Korbak et al., 2025; Baker et al., 2025; Chen et al., 2025a; Xiong et al., 2025; Arnav et al., 2025; Emmons et al., 2025)." |
| [Young 2026](./#p=young2026why) `young2026why#3` (monitorable vs faithful reasoning) | [Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) | partial | Meek et al. do distinguish the two, but the divergence they show runs the other way: CoTs that are faithful (acknowledge the cue) yet hard to monitor because they omit causal factors; since faithfulness is one of the two components of their monitorability score, they do not show monitorable but unfaithful reasoning. | "Our results show that models can appear faithful yet remain hard to monitor when they leave out key factors, and that monitorability differs sharply across models. [...] We combine faithfulness and verbosity into a single monitorability score that shows how well the CoT serves as the model’s external ‘working memory’" | "Meek et al. (2025) distinguished monitorability from faithfulness, showing that they can diverge: a model’s reasoning can be monitorable (detectable by a judge) without being faithful (accurately reflecting the model’s computation)." |
| [Yu et al. 2026](./#p=yu2026outcome) `yu2026outcome#4` (verifiability) | [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) | partial | Guan et al. support only the second clause, that monitoring reasoning traces helps detect misbehavior; they never define or use verifiability, and their monitors predict properties such as hint influence or reward hacking instead of checking a trace step by step ('verifiable' appears only for final answers in the process evaluations). | "We develop evaluations for monitorability, which we define as a monitor’s ability to accurately predict a specified property of an agent’s actions, e.g. whether the model is acting or will act harmfully. [...] Prior work has demonstrated CoT monitorability to have practical value in detecting misbehavior such as reward hacking during reinforcement learning [5]. [...] We find problems that have 1) a constrained set of valid and known solution paths, 2) hard-to-guess answers, and 3) easily verifiable answers" | "Verifiability asks whether a reasoning trace can be independently checked step-by-step, enabling monitoring for misalignment (Chan et al., 2025; Guan et al., 2025)." |
| [Zhou & May 2026](./#p=zhou2026conceptual) `zhou2026conceptual#8` (paraphrase defense (content-preserving paraphraser)) | [Roger & Greenblatt 2023](./#p=roger2023preventing) | partial | The source supports paraphrasing as an effective defense (context-aware paraphrasing holds hidden information to about 3 bits per KB), but it tested paraphrasing only against prompted and logit-watermarking schemes built from programs; its one learned (fine-tuned) scheme was never paraphrased, and fine-tuned schemes are left to future work. | "Training models to implement strong steganographic schemes requires a lot of resources and may miss advanced steganographic strategies, current or future models with long RLHF training runs, may find. Instead, we build steganographic encoders and decoders from arbitrary programs (with access to language models). [...] LLM watermarking techniques hide a few bits of information by slightly changing the output probabilities of the LLM. [...] We can also hide information in the generated text by modifying the prompt to include instructions about the information to hide and how to hide it. [...] Future work could develop better schemes, for example by fine-tuning models to achieve high hidden information density rather than simply prompting them or manipulating the logits. [...] when using context-aware paraphrasing and penalizing abnormal generations, it would be very difficult for language models to encode more than 3 bits of information per KB of text, thereby making encoded reasoning mostly useless." | "Prior work argues this defense is effective against learned token-level schemes (Roger and Greenblatt, 2023) [...] Roger and Greenblatt (2023) demonstrate this defense on a learned token-level scheme and argue that paraphrasing largely closes the channel." |
| [Zhou & Tao 2026](./#p=zhou2026nearzero) `zhou2026nearzero#6` (monitor (as a reward penalty)) | [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) | partial | Baker et al. do read the CoT with a monitor and put it in the RL reward, but they never subtract a suspiciousness score: the reward requires a trajectory to pass the unit tests and not trigger the monitor. | "We now explore the effect of using the monitor directly for optimization by updating the reward function to require that a trajectory not trigger the monitor in addition to passing all unit tests. [...] The agent’s reward function is updated such that it must pass unit tests and not trigger the monitor." | "Because reasoning models externalize much of their computation into readable text, one mitigation is to read that text: a monitor scores each rollout for suspiciousness and its score is subtracted from the reward (Baker et al., 2025; Korbak et al., 2025)." |

</details>

### Tensions

**Faithfulness: required, required only for the monitored property, or not required?**

[Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) reject the requirement: their notion "does not require a model’s externalized reasoning to faithfully reflect its internal reasoning process". [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) reject causal faithfulness as a requirement ("causal CoTs (see Figure 25) are not a necessary condition for monitorability") but keep faithfulness relative to the monitored property: "If a monitor $M$ can accurately predict property $v$ from latent $z$, then $z$ must be faithful with respect to $v$ and legible to $M$". [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) reject only completeness: "CoT does not need to completely represent the actual reasoning process in order to be a valuable additional safety layer".

On the other side, three papers state the requirement without restricting it to the monitored property: [Jia et al. 2026](./#p=jia2026faithfulness) (CoT is "useful for monitoring language models only when the reasoning trace faithfully reflects the computation that produces the final answer"), [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) (traces "should faithfully reflect the model’s internal decision-making path") and [Anonymous 2026 (one draw)](./#p=anon2026one) ("useful for safety only if the reasoning reflects what drives the model’s answers"). [Bhupatiraju & Nyaupane 2026](./#p=bhupatiraju2026stated) and [Shiromani et al. 2026](./#p=shiromani2026necessary) attribute it to CoT monitoring as an assumption ("assumes that the reasoning a model writes reflects the computation that directly produces its answer"; "assumes that a model’s written reasoning is informative about the factors that actually influenced its answer") without endorsing it. [Bürger et al. 2026](./#p=burger2026chain) and [Kaufmann et al. 2026](./#p=kaufmann2026aligned) take Baker et al.'s middle position, requiring the factors or computations the monitor needs and no more.

The direct disagreement is between Guan et al. and these three papers; Korbak et al. reject a complete representation, not faithfulness, and the check marks Korbak et al. supported for the faithfulness wordings of [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability), [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) and [Anonymous 2026 (one draw)](./#p=anon2026one).

**A property of the model, or of the model and the monitor?**

[Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) write that "”monitorability” refers to the intrinsic properties of the evaluated model, independent of the monitor’s specific capabilities", and [Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026monitorbench) give the monitor "exact decision-critical factors to look for", which they say "ensures monitorability reflects the properties of the evaluated LLM and is independent of the monitor’s specific capabilities". [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring), whose minimal-criterion score both papers use, call monitorability "a two-sided property" that depends on "the capacity of the monitor to extract useful information", and [Algaba et al. 2026](./#p=algaba2026reasoning), [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought), [Anonymous 2026 (improving monitorability)](./#p=anon2026improving) and [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) make the monitor part of the definition. Xiong et al.'s own score cuts against their statement: the same section defines g-mean² "with a higher score indicating better monitorability with respect to the applied monitor".

**Does necessity make a CoT monitorable?**

[Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) enforce necessity by making the bad behavior harder and state that "this forces the model to expose its reasoning, making it monitorable", while finding that models "can learn to obscure their intentions, but only when given significant help". [Schulz 2026](./#p=schulz2026monitor) draws the same consequence ("When the CoT is necessary for performing a task, the model must externalize its reasoning in some form."), and [Turpin et al. 2025](./#p=turpin2025teaching) a stronger one: where CoT is necessary, "a lack of verbalization would indicate the model is not reward hacking".

[Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) hold that "The externalized reasoning property does not guarantee monitorability", and [Anonymous 2026 (CoT reliance)](./#p=anon2026chainofthought) find that "monitorability errors can arise in other ways besides non-verbalization, including burying the verbalization within a long CoT that is difficult for the monitor to follow". [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing) give the information-theoretic version of the gap: the necessary condition $I(O;Z\mid X)>0$ "is implied by CoT being necessary for solving the task" but "can also be fulfilled in other cases, such as when CoT is a post-hoc explanation of the model output".

**Is omitting the evidence obfuscation?**

[Wang et al. 2026](./#p=wang2026monitorbench) separate the two: they expected models to "obscure decision-critical factors through strategies such as paraphrasing, switching languages, or unconventional encoding", and found that "the model largely omits explicit references to the decision-critical factors in its CoT, rather than obfuscating them". [Schulz 2026](./#p=schulz2026monitor) calls omission obfuscation (models that "still reward hack while not reasoning about it in their CoT" show "obfuscated reasoning"), and so do [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing) ("obfuscation via suppression: they learn to omit critical reasoning steps"). [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) draw the line elsewhere: they count it as obfuscation only when the monitor-aware model's undetected rate exceeds a no-reasoning baseline, so that "the model is actively using its CoT to perform the behavior while evading the monitor".

**Is obfuscation intentional?**

[Mao et al. 2026](./#p=mao2026taming) define CoT obfuscation as "an operational, non-intentional training outcome" and cite [Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring), [Drori et al. 2025](./#p=drori2025output) and [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) for it. The check marks the Haskins citation partial: Haskins et al. report that "models with both the means (CoT control) and motive (monitor knowledge) can obfuscate their reasoning, even without optimization pressure". [Korbak et al. 2025](./#p=korbak2025chain) write that "Future models may become able to avoid detection by overriding their propensity to think out loud, and, when reasoning is required, to deliberately obfuscate it", and [Jose et al. 2026](./#p=jose2026strategic) find that "intrinsic motivation to obfuscate leads to far better performance at evading a monitor than following instructions".

**Encoded from whom?**

[Lanham et al. 2023](./#p=lanham2023measuring) define encoded reasoning as "not understandable to human readers", and [Schulz 2026](./#p=schulz2026monitor) excludes from it reasoning that fools an LLM monitor but stays readable: "This does not fit the definition of encoded reasoning, as the reasoning is not hidden from humans". [Skaf et al. 2025](./#p=skaf2025large) define steganography as information "hidden to an external monitor" and require that "The monitor cannot decode the encodings, nor detect their presence", and [Anwar et al. 2026 (information theory)](./#p=anwar2026analyzing) call a CoT whose mistakes "the model knows to ignore ... but the monitor does not" "a weak form of steganography". Under Skaf et al.'s definition a load-bearing encoding that humans can read and the monitor cannot decode is steganographic; under Schulz's it is not encoded reasoning.

**Legible to whom?**

[Kirchner et al. 2024](./#p=kirchner2024proververifier) make legibility checkability by a verifier, with completeness and soundness both required: outputs are legible "if humans accept them as correct and can’t be fooled by similar-looking incorrect outputs". [Emmons et al. 2025 (pragmatic)](./#p=emmons2025pragmatic) make it readability by "a human with the same language capabilities as the user asking the model", so that switching languages "results in a Legibility score of 0", while [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) make it relative to the monitor: "if an agent’s CoT tokens are in French but the monitor only understands English, the monitor will fail due to illegibility", and "a different bilingual monitor would find it plausible". [Jiao et al. 2026](./#p=jiao2026tandem) replace checking by continuation ("whether a designated weaker generator can take over the senior’s partial trajectory and still complete the task successfully"), and [Roytburg et al. 2026](./#p=roytburg2026measuring) write that "Prior work defines legibility inconsistently"; the check marks Roytburg et al.'s restatement of Kirchner et al. partial, because Kirchner et al. call a prover legible only when completeness also holds.

**Deception, covert action, scheming, reward hacking and obfuscation: where are the lines?**

[Schoen et al. 2025](./#p=schoen2025stress) define covert actions so that "the definition of covert actions does not rely on intent on the AI system’s part", "in contrast to the related concept of deception (Ward et al., 2023)", while [Bu et al. 2026](./#p=bu2026spadebench) define deception by its effect, "behavior that induces false beliefs in humans or other intelligent systems", citing the same Ward et al. (2023). The scheming definitions differ in what they require: Schoen et al. "the covert pursuit of misaligned goals while hiding true capabilities and objectives"; [Google DeepMind 2026](./#p=google2026gemini4argon) a model that "knowingly and covertly pursues objectives misaligned with its developer's intention"; [Betley et al. 2025](./#p=betley2025tell) "strategically deceiving humans in order to further their goals"; [Emmons et al. 2025 (necessity)](./#p=emmons2025chain) models that "might strategically subvert human control"; and [Bu et al. 2026](./#p=bu2026spadebench) "long-horizon strategic goal pursuit, persistent hidden objectives, or coordinated planning across multiple episodes". Betley et al.'s gloss puts deceiving humans at the core, Google DeepMind's makes knowledge a condition, and Schoen et al. require covertness but not intent for the actions they measure; [Li et al. 2025](./#p=li2026spilling) treat the same notion as a capability, "the model’s ability to covertly pursue its own objective".

Reward hacking sits on both sides of the deception line. [Taufeeque et al. 2026](./#p=taufeeque2026obfuscation) list it among "Deceptive behaviors" and call reward-hacking policies that the probe catches "Blatant deception", and [Anonymous 2026 (honesty adapters)](./#p=anon2026traininghonesty) use School of Reward Hacks, where the assistant "either games an explicitly stated, gameable evaluation criterion or answers honestly", as a deception benchmark. [Baker et al. 2025](./#p=baker2025monitoring) and [Farquhar et al. 2025](./#p=farquhar2025mona) define reward hacking without reference to beliefs ("Exploiting flaws or misspecifications in learning objectives"; "achieves a high reward in a way the system designer would not want if they understood"), and Schoen et al. file it among the behavioral tags that "describe the type of scheming behavior being tested".

Obfuscation joins the three in the same experiments: in Taufeeque et al. a model trained against a deception detector "either remains honest, or becomes deceptive via two possible obfuscation strategies"; in [Haskins et al. 2026](./#p=haskins2026training) the behavior hidden from the monitor is deception about known product defects, and "detection is only counted over deceptive responses"; in [Jose et al. 2026](./#p=jose2026strategic) it is alignment-faking reasoning; and [Lee et al. 2026](./#p=lee2026training) train self-incrimination to make "covert misbehavior observable".

**Monitorable without a monitor?**

[Meek et al. 2025](./#p=meek2025measuring) score monitorability as "the average between the faithfulness and verbosity metrics", a score built from verbalization rates rather than from a monitor's predictions, and [Occhipinti et al. 2026](./#p=occhipinti2026probing) count as monitorable "all traces where the hint’s influence is visible". [Yang et al. 2025](./#p=yang2025investigating) report "a gap between being monitorable and being monitored correctly": "Even when CoT is always faithful, the monitor itself may fail to strike the right balance." [Young 2026](./#p=young2026why) restates Meek et al. as showing reasoning "monitorable (detectable by a judge) without being faithful"; the check marks this partial, because Meek et al. show the reverse divergence: "models can appear faithful yet remain hard to monitor when they leave out key factors".

**What does the minimal-criterion score measure?**

[Guan et al. 2025](./#p=guan2025monitoring) define the minimal-criterion rate from the requirement that "the monitor should trigger at least as often as this rate in the intervention arm", and six papers reuse it. [Anonymous 2026 (improving monitorability)](./#p=anon2026improving) prove that it is the upper end of the bounds on causal recall, so it bounds causal recall from above and does not give its value, and they drop the control-arm specificity from Guan et al.'s composite ($\text{g-mean}^2_{\text{int}}:=\mathrm{TPR}_{\mathrm{mc}}\cdot\mathrm{TNR}_{X=1}$). [Duzan & Cooper Stickland 2026](./#p=duzan2026chainofthought) keep both specificities but multiply them with the plain detection rate on behavioral-shift cases, and [Xiong et al. 2026](./#p=xiong2026monitorability) warn of a "false sense of monitorability": "a model might achieve a high $\text{g-mean}^{2}$ score simply by overfitting to the prompt’s constraints".
